P REFACE

These notes form a comprehensive, second-year introduction to probability. The Semester 1, 2026
STAT2003/STAT7003 course follows these notes closely.
The notes evolved from previous notes create by Emeritus Prof. Dirk Kroese who has written
multiple books about probability, simulation, statistics, machine learning, data science, and related
fields. The current notes are edited by A/Prof. Yoni Nazarathy and Dr Kazutoshi (Kasu) Yamazaki.
In addition to the content in these notes, any material discussed in the lectures and given in
the tutorial problem sets is fair game for the exam. A few selected sections or subsections will be
omitted from the exam material, as announced towards the end of the semester. The distribution
formula table in the appendix at the end of these notes is supplied with the exam.
To completely master STAT2003/STAT7003 it is important that you
1. visit the lectures, where the lecturing staff provides many extra examples;
2. do the tutorial exercises and the exercises in the appendix, which are there to help you with
the “technical” side of things; you will learn here to apply the concepts learned at the lectures,
3. carry out random experiments on the computer, in the simulation project. This will give you
a better intuition about how randomness works.
4. read these notes and make sure you can reproduce the examples and derivations (proofs).
All of these will be essential if you wish to understand probability beyond “filling in the formulas”.

5

Preface

6

Structure of the Course
The course is comprised of 10 units, with each unit having an associated chapter in these notes.
Chapter 1 - Random Experiments, Probability Spaces, and Simulation: In this chapter we
introduce the notion of random experiments and probability. With this we present a simplified
mathematical description of the probability triple and study simple examples. The probability
axioms and resulting properties are studied. We also see how random experiments can be simulated
on a computer, a topic sometimes called Monte-Carlo.
Chapter 2 - Counting, Inclusion-Exclusion, and Independence: We continue the study of basic
probability by reviewing basics of combinatorics, namely counting techniques. These approaches
are needed when considering equiprobable probability spaces, and related cases. We also study
the probability of a union of an arbitrary finite number of events via the so-called inclusionexclusion formula. Finally we define the notion of independent events.
Chapter 3 - Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments: In this chapter
we consider the general notion of random variables, focusing on discrete valued random variables. With this we study their distributions, and moment descriptors, including expectation, mean,
and variance. In particular, we study families of random variables associated with independent
Bernoulli (coin-flip) trials. This includes the binomial, geometric, and negative Binomial distributions. We also visit the Poisson distribution and the hypergeometric distribution.
Chapter 4 - Conditional Probability and Markov Chains: In this chapter we introduce basics
of conditional probability. With this we visit the law of total probability and Bayes’ theorem. We
also revisit some aspects of discrete random variables from the previous chapter, now through the
lens of conditional probability. We also visit the product rule and then introduce the concept of
Markov chains. These stochastic processes are a sequence of discrete random variables that exhibit
the Markov property. In their elementary study, we see how matrix multiplication is inherently
related to their probability laws, and we visit basic concepts such as their limiting distribution at an
introductory exploratory level.
Chapter 5 - Continuous Random Variables and their Distributions: In this chapter we introduce continuous distributions, their expectation, moments, and properties. We focus on families
of continuous distributions including the Normal distribution, exponential, uniform, Gamma, Beta,
Pareto, Cauchy, and a few others.
Chapter 6 - More Aspects of Random Variables and Distributions: In this chapter we explore
several additional aspects of random variables and their distributions. We develop techniques for
finding the distribution of a transformed random variable, and see how relationships between families of probability distributions arise. We then consider the inverse probability transform, a universal method for simulating random variables. We establish the Markov and Chebyshev inequalities,
which bound tail probabilities using only moments. Finally, we encounter random variables whose
moments are infinite or even undefined — the Pareto and Cauchy distributions.
Chapter 7 - Joint Distributions: In previous chapters we studied individual random variables —
their distributions, moments, and transformations. In this chapter we consider situations where two
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Preface

7

or more random variables are observed simultaneously. We develop the theory of joint distributions,
marginal and conditional distributions, covariance and correlation, and the multivariate normal and
multinomial distributions. We then introduce conditional expectation and the tower property (law
of total expectation), and apply these tools to random sums and the law of total variance.
Chapter 8 - More Aspects of Joint Distributions: In this chapter we study transformations of vector random variables — their densities via the Jacobian, linear maps and their effects on mean and
covariance, and the Cholesky decomposition for generating multivariate normal samples. We then
derive distributions of sums, products, ratios, and order statistics of independent random variables,
with convolutions as a central tool.
Chapter 9 - Moment Generating Functions, Related Transforms, and Examples: In this
chapter we introduce probability generating functions and moment generating functions — transforms that encode a distribution and turn convolutions into simple algebra. We develop their properties, show how to extract probabilities and moments, and apply them to sums of independent
random variables and a branching process model.
Chapter 10 - Random Samples and Limit Theorems: In this chapter we study statistics computed from random samples — the sample mean and sample variance, their distributions, and their
unbiasedness. We then establish the laws of large numbers and the central limit theorem, which
together describe the behaviour of averages and sums for large samples.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Preface

Code Examples in These Notes
This is a course about probability, not about computer programming. The emphasis throughout is
on understanding probability concepts, distributions, derivations, and problem solving. Nevertheless, using a computer language as an aid for understanding probabilistic ideas is important, and
for this reason we include code examples in many of the sections. Some topics, particularly Monte
Carlo simulation and random number generation, directly require code to illustrate and explore.
All code examples use Python, but the ideas are what matter, not the specific language. Other
languages such as Julia or R would have worked just as well, if not better in some cases. You are
encouraged to copy the code snippets, run them, and modify them to explore. Experimenting with
the code (changing parameters, increasing sample sizes, trying different distributions) is one of the
best ways to build intuition about randomness and probability.
Large language models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Claude are also useful tools for generating, explaining, and debugging code. To support this, we include an LLM prompt at the bottom of
every code snippet: a short natural-language description that, if given to an LLM, would produce
code similar to the snippet. You can use these prompts as a starting point and iterate with an LLM
to explore variations.
Regarding assessment: students are not required to write code for the exam. However, you
are expected to understand basic Python code in the context of random number generation and
simulation. This does not mean memorising the details of specific libraries or function names
(such details would be provided in an exam setting if needed). Rather, it means understanding the
principles: what it means to generate a uniform random number, how a simulation loop works, how
sample means are computed, and so on.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

CHAPTER

1

R ANDOM E XPERIMENTS , P ROBABILITY
S PACES , AND S IMULATION

In this chapter we introduce the notion of random experiments and probability. With this we present
a simplified mathematical description of the probability triple and study simple examples. The
probability axioms and resulting properties are studied. We also see how random experiments can
be simulated on a computer, a topic sometimes called Monte-Carlo.

1.1

Random Experiments, Reality, and Simulation

The basic notion in probability is that of a random experiment: an experiment whose outcome
cannot be determined in advance, but is nevertheless still subject to analysis.
Examples of random experiments are:
1. tossing a die,
2. measuring the amount of rainfall in Brisbane in January,
3. counting the number of calls arriving at a telephone exchange during a fixed time period,
4. selecting a random sample of fifty people and observing the number of left-handers,
5. choosing at random ten people and measuring their height.
These examples illustrate random processes that occur in the real world. However, each can
also be simulated on a computer to study their behaviors without having to physically repeat each
experiment multiple times.
The most fundamental stochastic experiment is the experiment where a
coin is tossed a number of times, say n times. Indeed, much of probability theory can be based

Example (Coin Tossing).

9

Random Experiments, Probability Spaces, and Simulation

10

on this simple experiment, as we shall see in subsequent chapters. To better understand how this
experiment behaves, we can carry it out in reality by physically tossing a coin, or simulate it on a
digital computer using Python. This duality allows us to both observe and model the behavior of
coin tosses more efficiently and repeatedly.
The following simple Python program simulates a sequence of 100 tosses with a fair coin (that
is, heads and tails are equally likely), and plots the results in a bar chart.
Simulating 100 fair coin tosses

LLM Prompt > Write Python code that simulates 100 fair coin tosses (as 0s and 1s) using uniform
random numbers and displays the results as a bar chart.

Here x is a vector with 1s and 0s, indicating Heads and Tails, say. Running this code produces
the bar chart in Figure 1.1: blue bars indicate “Heads” (=1) and green bars indicate “Tails” (=0).

Figure 1.1: Simulated 100 fair coin tosses with seed 2026. Blue bars are “Heads” (=1), green bars
are “Tails” (=0).
Notice the line np.random.seed(2026) in the code. This sets the random seed, which determines the starting point for the pseudorandom number generator. Every time you run the code
with the same seed, you get exactly the same sequence of “random” numbers, and hence the same
sequence of coin tosses. This is useful for reproducibility. We say more about pseudorandom
number generation in Section 1.4.
What happens if we change the seed? The following code is identical except that the seed is set
to 2027 instead of 2026.
Simulating 100 fair coin tosses (different seed)

LLM Prompt > Write Python code that simulates 100 fair coin tosses with a different random seed and

displays the results as a bar chart.

The result is shown in Figure 1.2. The pattern of Heads and Tails is completely different from
Figure 1.1. Setting two different seeds is analogous to tossing the 100 coins on two separate ocCopyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

casions: each run produces a fresh, independent-looking sequence. If we do not specify a seed at
all, Python uses whatever value happens to be in the system (based on the current time or operating
system entropy), so each run automatically gives a different result.

Figure 1.2: Simulated 100 fair coin tosses with seed 2027. The pattern differs from Figure 1.1, just
as two separate physical experiments would differ.
We can also plot the running average of “Heads” against the number of tosses. The following
code carries out three separate experiments (with seeds 2026, 2027, and 2028) and plots the running
average for each.
Running average of heads over n tosses

LLM Prompt > Write Python code that runs three coin-toss experiments (with different seeds), com-

putes the running average of heads for each, and plots all three trajectories on one figure with a
dashed line at 1/2.

The result is depicted in Figure 1.3. Notice that the average number of Heads seems to converge
to 21 , but there is a lot of random fluctuation, especially for small n.

Figure 1.3: The running average of heads in n tosses for three experiments, where n = 1, . . . , 100.
The dashed line is at 12 .
Control charts, see Figure 1.4, are frequently used in manufacturing as
a method for quality control. In reality, each hour the average output of the process is measured —
for example, the average weight of 10 bags of sugar — to assess if the process is still “in control”,
i.e., if the machine still puts on average the correct amount of sugar in the bags. Simulations can
also be used to predict behaviors of production processes under different configurations and identify
potential issues before they occur on the shop floor. When the process > Upper Control Limit or <
Lower Control Limit, an alarm is raised to indicate that the process is out of control, for instance,
because the machine either puts too much or not enough sugar in the bags. The question is how to
set the control limits, since the random process naturally fluctuates around its “center” or “target”
line.

A 4-engine aeroplane is able to fly on just one engine on each wing. In
reality, this can be considered by observing the function of the engines over specified periods to
assess reliability. Simulations are pivotal for risk assessment and to calculate the probabilities
involved without endangering lives or the actual aircraft. All engines are unreliable.

during a specified period of time. There are 24 = 16 possible outcomes of the experiment. Which
outcomes lead to “system failure”? Moreover, if the P of failure within some time period is known
for each of the engines, what is the P of failure for the entire system? Again this can be viewed as
a random experiment.

We wish to describe these experiments via appropriate mathematical models. These models
consist of three building blocks: a sample space, a set of events, and a probability. We will now
describe each of these objects.

1.2

Events and Sets

This section reviews the set theory concepts that underpin probability and introduces the language
of events.

Quick Review of Set Theory
A set S is a collection of distinct objects called elements; we write x ∈ S to mean x belongs to
S . The empty set ∅ contains no elements, and the universal set Ω contains all elements under
consideration. A set A is a subset of B (written A ⊆ B) if every element of A also belongs to B.
The basic set operations are:
• Union: A ∪ B = {x ∈ Ω : x ∈ A or x ∈ B} — the set of elements belonging to A, or B, or
both.
• Intersection: A ∩ B = {x ∈ Ω : x ∈ A and x ∈ B} — the set of elements belonging to both A
and B.
• Complement: Ac = {x ∈ Ω : x < A} — the set of elements in Ω that do not belong to A.
• Set difference: A \ B = {x ∈ Ω : x ∈ A and x < B} — the set of elements in A that do not
belong to B. Note that A \ B = A ∩ Bc .
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

The Power Set
The power set of a set S , denoted P(S ), is the set of all subsets of S (including ∅ and S itself). If
S has n elements, then P(S ) has 2n elements.
If S = {1, 2, 3}, the power set is

Events
In probability, the sample space Ω plays the role of the universal set: it contains all possible
outcomes of an experiment. Events are subsets of Ω, denoted by capital letters A, B, C, . . .. An
event A occurs if the experimental outcome belongs to A. For example:
• The event that the sum of two dice is 10 or more: A = {(4, 6), (5, 5), (5, 6), (6, 4), (6, 5), (6, 6)}.
• The event that a machine lives less than 1000 days: A = [0, 1000).
Set operations translate directly: A ∪ B means “A or B (or both) occur”, A ∩ B means “A and B
S
both occur”, and Ac means “A does not occur”. For indexed families, i∈I Ai is the event that at
T
least one Ai occurs, and i∈I Ai that all Ai occur. Two events are disjoint (or mutually exclusive)
if A ∩ B = ∅. If A ⊆ B, we say that A implies B: whenever A occurs, the outcome belongs to A and
hence also to B, so B must occur as well.
Example (Coin Tossing).

Suppose a coin is tossed three times, recording each head (H) and tail

(T ). The sample space is:
Ω = {HHH, HHT, HT H, HT T, T HH, T HT, T T H, T T T } .

(1.6)

The event A that the third toss is heads is:
A = {HHH, HT H, T HH, T T H} .

Example (Casting Two Dice).

(1.7)

Suppose we cast two dice. The sample space is:

Ω = {(1, 1), (1, 2), . . . , (1, 6), (2, 1), . . . , (6, 6)} .

(1.8)

Let A = {(6, 1), . . . , (6, 6)} be the event that the first die is 6, and let B = {(1, 6), . . . , (6, 6)} be the
event that the second die is 6. Then,
A ∩ B = {(6, 1), . . . , (6, 6)} ∩ {(1, 6), . . . , (6, 6)} = {(6, 6)}

(1.9)

is the event that both dice show 6.

The Probability Triple, Axioms, and Properties

In Section 1.2 we described sample spaces and events using the language of set theory. We now
formalize what it means to assign probabilities to events. Ideas of probability emerged as early
as the 16th and 17th centuries through the work of mathematicians such as Cardano, Pascal, and
Fermat, yet the field was only placed on rigorous mathematical foundations in the 20th century,
when A.N. Kolmogorov formulated his axioms using the language of measure theory.
The central object is the probability triple (Ω, F , P ). Its three components are:
• Ω, the sample space — the set of all possible outcomes (introduced in Section 1.1),
• F , the collection of events — the subsets of Ω to which we assign probabilities,
• P , the probability measure — a function that assigns a probability to each event in F .
For the purposes of this course, we can conceptually think of F as the power set P(Ω) — the
collection of all subsets of Ω. Yet in a more advanced course, we may take F to be a Borel sigmaalgebra — something you learn about in STAT3004 and/or a measure theory course. This subtlety
is discussed briefly in the subsection on sigma-algebras at the end of this section.
Definition : Probability Triple — A probability triple (or probability space) is a triple (Ω, F , P )

where:
• Ω is the sample space,
• F is a collection of subsets of Ω (the events),
• P : F → [0, 1] is a probability measure satisfying Kolmogorov’s axioms below.

That is, P is a function that operates on subsets (events) of Ω and returns a real value between
0 and 1. The higher the value, the more probability we attribute to the event. The probability
measure P must satisfy certain fundamental rules, known as Kolmogorov’s axioms, which ensure
that probability behaves in a consistent and mathematically rigorous way.
Definition : Kolmogorov’s Axioms — A function P : F → [0, 1] is a probability measure if it

satisfies:
1. Non-negativity: P (A) ⩾ 0 for every event A ∈ F .
2. Normalization: P (Ω) = 1.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

18

Random Experiments, Probability Spaces, and Simulation
3. Finite additivity: If A1 , . . . , An are finitely many disjoint events in F , then

Note that countable additivity implies finite additivity (set Ai = ∅ for i > n), so axiom 4 is the
stronger statement. However, finite additivity is listed separately because it is the version used most
often in practice.
From these four axioms alone, a rich collection of properties can be derived. The following
claim lists the most fundamental ones.
Claim 2: Properties of Probability — Let A and B be events. Then,

1. P (∅) = 0.
2. Monotonicity: A ⊆ B =⇒ P (A) ⩽ P (B).
3. P (A) ⩽ 1.
4. Complement Rule: P (Ac ) = 1 − P (A).
5. Inclusion-Exclusion: P (A ∪ B) = P (A) + P (B) − P (A ∩ B).

Proof:
1. Since Ω = Ω ∪ ∅, we have by the sum rule P (Ω) = P (Ω) + P (∅), and therefore, by the second
axiom, 1 = 1 + P (∅), from which it follows that P (∅) = 0.
2. If A ⊆ B, then B = A ∪ (B ∩ Ac ), where A and B ∩ Ac are disjoint. Hence, by the sum rule,
P (B) = P (A) + P (B ∩ Ac ), which is (by the first axiom) greater than or equal to P (A).
3. This follows directly from property 2 and axiom 2, since A ⊆ Ω.
4. Ω = A ∪ Ac , where A and Ac are disjoint. Hence, by the sum rule and axiom 2: 1 = P (Ω) =
P (A) + P (Ac ), and thus P (Ac ) = 1 − P (A).
5. Write A ∪ B as the disjoint union of A and B ∩ Ac . Then P (A ∪ B) = P (A) + P (B ∩ Ac ). Also,
B = (A ∩ B) ∪ (B ∩ Ac ), so that P (B) = P (A ∩ B) + P (B ∩ Ac ). Combining these two equations
gives P (A ∪ B) = P (A) + P (B) − P (A ∩ B).
□
Suppose a fair six-sided die is rolled. Let A = {2, 4, 6} (even) and B =
{1, 2, 3} (at most 3). Then P (A) = P (B) = 21 . By the complement rule, P (Ac ) = 1 − 12 = 21 . By

Example (Rolling a Die).

Sigma-Algebras
We said that F is “the collection of events” to which we assign probabilities. But what qualifies as
a valid F ? The answer is that F must be a sigma-algebra.
Definition : Sigma-Algebra — A collection F of subsets of Ω is called a sigma-algebra (or σ-

algebra) if:
1. Ω ∈ F .
2. If A ∈ F , then Ac ∈ F

(closed under complementation).

Example (Sigma-algebras on Ω = {1, 2, 3}).

Consider Ω = {1, 2, 3}. The power set


P(Ω) = ∅, {1}, {2}, {3}, {1, 2}, {1, 3}, {2, 3}, {1, 2, 3}

(1.14)

is a sigma-algebra (all 8 subsets are included, so any complement or union of members is again a
member).

A smaller collection can also qualify. Consider Fe = ∅, {1}, {2, 3}, {1, 2, 3} . We verify the
three conditions:
1. Ω = {1, 2, 3} ∈ Fe. ✓
2. Complements: {1}c = {2, 3} ∈ Fe, {2, 3}c = {1} ∈ Fe, ∅c = {1, 2, 3} ∈ Fe, and {1, 2, 3}c = ∅ ∈ Fe.
✓
3. Unions: every union of members of Fe is again in Fe (for instance, {1}∪{2, 3} = {1, 2, 3} ∈ Fe).
✓
Both P(Ω) and Fe are valid choices for the event collection in a probability triple on this specific
Ω. If we use the power set P(Ω), then every subset of Ω is an event and we can speak about its
probability. But if we use the smaller Fe, then the subsets {2}, {3}, {1, 2}, and {1, 3} are not in Fe and
are therefore not events — we cannot assign probabilities to them.
Why sigma-algebras? For finite (or countable) sample spaces, the power set P(Ω) always works
as F , and there is no issue. The subtlety arises for uncountable sample spaces such as Ω = R.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Random Experiments, Probability Spaces, and Simulation

21

In that setting, it can be shown that no probability measure satisfying Kolmogorov’s axioms can
be consistently defined on all subsets of Ω (i.e., the full power set). To guarantee that a valid
probability measure exists, we must restrict to a smaller, well-behaved collection of subsets — a
sigma-algebra. This is the central insight of the measure-theoretic formulation of probability.
The formal definition of a sigma-algebra is not used further in this course. For our purposes, F
can be conceptually thought of as the power set.

Continuity of Probability
Continuity of probability is a slightly more advanced property that connects limits of sequences of
events to limits of their probabilities. It is a direct consequence of countable additivity.

Continuity of probability is not used further in this course, but it illustrates the power of the

1.4

More on Simulation

In Section 1.1 we saw how to simulate simple experiments such as coin tosses and dice rolls. We
now develop the ideas behind computer-based random number generation more carefully.

The Uniform Distribution on [0, 1]
In Section 1.3 we introduced the probability triple (Ω, F , P ). Consider the specific case where the
sample space is the unit interval, Ω = [0, 1], and the probability of any sub-interval [a, b] ⊆ [0, 1]
is simply its length:
P ([a, b]) = b − a .

(1.20)

This is called the uniform distribution on [0, 1], written U(0, 1). Intuitively, it describes the experiment of “picking a point at random” from the interval [0, 1], with no part of the interval favoured
over any other. Continuous probability distributions will be treated formally in Chapter 5; for now,
this intuitive description is sufficient.
The U(0, 1) distribution is the fundamental building block of computer-based simulation. A
computer can generate numbers that behave as if they were drawn independently from this probability space. Given such samples, we can estimate the probability of any event A ⊆ [0, 1]: generate
a large number of samples, check how many fall in A, and compute the proportion. By the law of
large numbers (Chapter 10), this proportion converges to P (A) as the number of samples grows.
This idea — using random sampling to estimate quantities that may be difficult to compute analytically — is the essence of Monte Carlo simulation, named after the famed Monaco casino.
Applications span cryptography, financial modeling, physical sciences, and engineering.

Pseudorandom Sequence Algorithms
But how does a computer, a deterministic machine, produce numbers that “look random”? Early
simulation relied on manual techniques (coin flipping, dice rolling) or physical devices (noise diodes). Modern approaches use deterministic algorithms called pseudorandom number generators
that are fast, reproducible, and pass statistical tests for randomness. The most classical family is
the linear congruential generator, which produces a sequence via
Xi+1 = a Xi + c

(mod m) ,

(1.21)

where X0 is the seed, and a, c, m are positive integers called the multiplier, increment, and modulus.
The modulo-m operation takes the remainder after dividing by m, so each Xi ∈ {0, 1, . . . , m − 1}.
The pseudorandom numbers
Xi
Ui =
,
(1.22)
m
then approximate independent U(0, 1) random variables.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Random Experiments, Probability Spaces, and Simulation
Example (Linear Congruential Generator).

Let a = 3, c = 2, m = 7, and X0 = 1. Applying (1.21)

repeatedly:
i

The sequence has period 6 (then repeats). The choice of a, c, m strongly affects quality; numbertheoretic results guide good choices. In practice, m is chosen as a large prime fitting the computer
word size (e.g. m = 231 − 1 for 32-bit systems).
Linear congruential generators are now considered outdated for serious simulation work, due to
short periods and known statistical deficiencies (for example, consecutive outputs can exhibit lattice
structure in higher dimensions). Modern pseudorandom number generators include the Mersenne
Twister (period 219937 − 1, used by default in many languages including Python’s random module), the Permuted Congruential Generator (PCG, used by NumPy since version 1.17), and the
xoshiro/xoroshiro family. These algorithms offer vastly longer periods, better statistical properties,
and faster performance. Their design and analysis are beyond the scope of this course.
Most languages provide a built-in pseudorandom generator. In Python, the rand function from
numpy.random produces U(0, 1) samples.

Generating Bernoulli Random Variables
A Bernoulli random variable (formally defined in Chapter 3) takes the value 1 with probability p
and 0 with probability 1 − p. Since rand() produces a U(0, 1) value, the comparison rand() <=
p returns True (which Python treats as 1) with probability p. We can use this to simulate coin flips
and other binary outcomes.
Generating Bernoulli random variables with p = 0.3

We continue the study of basic probability by reviewing basics of combinatorics, namely counting
techniques. These approaches are needed when considering equiprobable probability spaces,
and related cases. We also study the probability of a union of an arbitrary finite number of events via
the so-called inclusion-exclusion formula. Finally we define the notion of independent events.

2.1

Equiprobable Probability Spaces

For a set S , we write |S | for the cardinality of the set, which counts the number of elements in the
set. When the set is finite, this is a non-negative integer. For example, |∅| = 0 and |{a, b, c}| = 3.
The most basic way one can think of probability—but certainly not the only way—is to assume
that each outcome in the sample space Ω is equally likely to occur. Such spaces are called equiprobable probability spaces. When |Ω| is finite and all outcomes are equally likely, the probability
of an event A is given by:
|A|
(2.1)
|Ω|
where |A| denotes the number of outcomes in A and |Ω| the total number of outcomes in the sample
space. This simple formula reduces computing probabilities to a counting problem: determine |A|
and |Ω|.

These examples had small sample spaces where counting |A| and |Ω| was straightforward —
we could simply list all outcomes. But what happens when the sample space is large? The next
example shows that even a natural equiprobable model can lead to counting challenges that require
systematic techniques.
Suppose there are n people in a room. Assuming that each
person’s birthday is equally likely to fall on any of the 365 days of the year (ignoring leap years),
what is the probability that at least two people share a birthday?
Example (The Birthday Problem).

The sample space Ω consists of all possible birthday assignments for the n people. Since each
person can have any of 365 birthdays, |Ω| = 365n .
Let A be the event that at least two people share a birthday. It is easier to compute P (Ac ), the
probability that all n birthdays are different. For all birthdays to be distinct, the first person can
have any of 365 days, the second any of the remaining 364, and so on:

Uniform Continuous Sampling
The equilikely principle above applies when the sample space Ω is finite. However, a similar idea
works when Ω is a continuous region — a line segment, a region in the plane, a volume in threedimensional space, and so on — provided there is a well-defined notion of “size” (length, area,
volume, etc.). We will return to continuous distributions and random variables in Chapter 3 and
beyond; for now, we simply note that one can sample a point “uniformly at random” from a region
Ω, meaning that every position in Ω is equally likely.
In this setting, the probability triple (Ω, F , P ) is specified as follows:
• Ω is the region from which we sample (e.g., a disk, a rectangle, an interval).
• F is a suitable collection of subsets of Ω (for our purposes, any “reasonable” geometric
region is an event).
• P assigns probabilities via the ratio of sizes:
Size(A)
,
Size(Ω)
where Size(·) denotes length, area, or volume as appropriate.
P (A) =

This is the continuous analogue of P (A) = |A|/|Ω|: cardinality is replaced by a geometric
measure of size.

In the next section we develop systematic counting methods — permutations and combinations

2.2

Counting Techniques and Combinatorics

Counting is not always easy. Let us first look at some examples:
1. A multiple choice form has 20 questions; each question has 3 choices. In how many possible
ways can the exam be completed? Answer: 3,486,784,401.
2. Consider a horse race with 8 horses. How many ways are there to gamble on the placings
(1st, 2nd, 3rd). Answer: 336.
3. Jessica has a collection of 20 CDs1 , she wants to take 3 of them to work. How many possibilities does she have? Answer: 1140.
4. How many different throws are possible with 3 identically looking dice? Here a throw is
considered a result on the three dice, e.g., (1, 1, 5), (2, 3, 6), (4, 4, 4), etc. Answer: 56.
Solving counting problems requires experience and practice, and some remain exceedingly
hard. Many, however, can be cast into a simple framework.
1

in enormous shelving units, only to later rip them all to MP3s and wonder why they kept the shelves.

The Four Cases of Elementary Counting
Fortunately, many counting problems can be cast into the simple framework of drawing balls from
an urn (Figure 2.2a). Consider an urn with n different balls, numbered 1, . . . , n from which k balls
are drawn. This can be done in a number of different ways. First, the balls can be drawn one-byone, or one could draw all the k balls at the same time. In the first case the order in which the balls
are drawn can be noted, in the second case that is not possible. In the latter case we can (and will)
still assume the balls are drawn one-by-one, but that the order is not noted.
Second, once a ball is drawn, it can either be put back into the urn (after the number is recorded),
or left out. This is called, respectively, drawing with and without replacement. All in all there are
4 possible experiments: (ordered, with replacement), (ordered, without replacement), (unordered,
without replacement) and (unordered, with replacement). An equivalent viewpoint is to distribute
k balls into n labelled bins (Figure 2.2b).

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Counting, Inclusion-Exclusion, and Independence

Figure 2.2: Two equivalent views of counting problems. (a) Draw k balls from an urn containing n
distinguishable balls; the choices of replacement and ordering yield four counting cases. (b) Equivalently, place k balls into n labelled bins; the choices of ball distinguishability and bin capacity
yield the same four cases.
The art is to recognise a seemingly unrelated counting problem as one of these four urn problems. For the 4 examples above we have the following
1. Example 1 (multiple choice) can be viewed as drawing k = 20 balls from an urn containing
n = 3 balls, noting the order, and with replacement. Equivalently, place k = 20 differentcolour balls into n = 3 bins with no limit per bin.
2. Example 2 (horse race) is equivalent to drawing k = 3 balls from an urn containing n = 8
balls, noting the order, and without replacement. Equivalently, place k = 3 different-colour
balls into n = 8 bins with at most one ball per bin.
3. Example 3 (CDs) is a case of taking k = 3 balls from an urn containing n = 20 balls, not
noting the order, and without replacement. Equivalently, place k = 3 same-colour balls into
n = 20 bins with at most one ball per bin.
4. Example 4 (dice) is a case of drawing k = 3 balls from an urn containing n = 6 balls, not
noting the order, and with replacement. Equivalently, place k = 3 same-colour balls into
n = 6 bins with no limit per bin.
Before we proceed it is important to introduce a notation that reflects whether the outcomes/arrangements are ordered or not. In particular, we denote ordered arrangements by vectors, e.g.,
(1, 2, 3) , (3, 2, 1), and unordered arrangements by sets, e.g., {1, 2, 3} = {3, 2, 1}. Each case can
be viewed from two equivalent perspectives: the sampling from an urn view and the balls-intobins view (distributing balls into labelled bins). The table below shows both; bold labels give the
sampling perspective and parenthetical annotations give the balls-into-bins perspective. Differentcolour balls correspond to ordered sampling (each ball carries its draw number), same-colour to

The Size of the Power Set
Recall from Chapter 1 that the power set P(S ) of a set S is the collection of all subsets of S . If
S has n elements, then |P(S )| = 2n . To see why, note that forming a subset of S = {s1 , . . . , sn }
amounts to making n independent yes/no decisions — for each element, either include it or not.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Counting, Inclusion-Exclusion, and Independence

There are 2 choices per element and n elements, giving 2n subsets in total. This is the reason 2S is
sometimes used as notation for the power set.

The binomial coefficient nk counts the number of subsets of size k from a set of n elements.
Since every subset has some size k ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n}, the total number of subsets is

setting a = b = 1 in the binomial theorem. In other words,

the binomial theorem (1 + 1)n = nk=0 nk is a counting statement: the left side counts all subsets by
the multiplication principle, while the right side counts them grouped by size.

More examples
Here are some more examples. Not all problems can be directly related to the 4 problems above.
Some require additional reasoning. However, the counting principles remain the same.
1. In how many ways can the numbers 1,. . . ,5 be arranged, such as 13524, 25134, etc?
Answer: 5! = 120.
2. How many different arrangements are there of the numbers 1,2,. . . ,7, such that the first 3
numbers are 1,2,3 (in any order) and the last 4 numbers are 4,5,6,7 (in any order)?
Answer: 3! × 4!.
3. How many different arrangements are there of the word “arrange”, such as “aarrnge”, “arrngea”, etc?
Answer: Convert this into a ball drawing problem with 7 balls, numbered 1,. . . ,7. Balls 1 and
2 correspond to ’a’, balls 3 and 4 to ’r’, ball 5 to ’n’, ball 6 to ’g’ and ball 7 to ’e’. The total
number of permutations of the numbers is 7!. However, since, for example, (1,2,3,4,5,6,7) is
identical to (2,1,3,4,5,6,7) (when substituting the letters back), we must divide 7! by 2! × 2!
to account for the 4 ways the two ’a’s and ’r’s can be arranged. So the answer is 7!/4 = 1260.
4. An urn has 1000 balls, labelled 000, 001, . . . , 999. How many balls are there that have all
numbers in ascending order (for example 047 and 489, but not 033 or 321)?
Answer: There are 10 × 9 × 8 = 720 balls with different numbers. Each triple of numbers can
be arranged in 3! = 6 ways, and only one of these is in ascending order. So the total number
of balls in ascending order is 720/6 = 120.
5. In a group of 20 people each person has a different birthday. How many different arrangements of these birthdays are there (assuming each year has 365 days)?
Answer: 365!
.
345!

Back to Equiprobable Probability Spaces
Once we’ve learned how to count, we can apply the equilikely principle to calculate probabilities.
Here are some examples:
1. What is the probability that out of a group of 40 people all have different birthdays?
Answer: Choosing the birthdays is like choosing 40 balls with replacement from an urn
containing the balls 1,. . . ,365. Thus, our sample space Ω consists of vectors of length 40,
whose components are chosen from {1, . . . , 365}. There are |Ω| = 36540 such vectors possible,
and all are equally likely. Let A be the event that all 40 people have different birthdays. Then,
|A| = 365!/325!. It follows that P (A) = |A|/|Ω| ≈ 0.109, so not very big!
2. What is the probability that in 10 tosses with a fair coin we get exactly 5 Heads and 5 Tails?
Answer: Here Ω consists of vectors of length 10 consisting of 1s (Heads) and 0s (Tails),
so there are 210 of them, and all are equally likely. Let A be the event of exactly 5 heads.
We must count how many binary vectors there are with exactly 5 1s. This is equivalent to
determining
of the 5 1s can be chosen out of 10 positions,
10 in how many ways the10positions

10
that is, 5 . Consequently, P (A) = 5 /2 = 252/1024 ≈ 0.25.
3. We draw at random 13 cards from a full deck of cards. What is the probability that we draw
4 Hearts and 3 Diamonds?
Answer: Give the cards a number from 1 to 52. Suppose 1–13 is Hearts, 14–26 is Diamonds,
etc. Ω consists
of unordered sets of size 13, without repetition, e.g., {1, 2, . . . , 13}. There are

52
|Ω| = 13 of these sets, and they are all equally likely. Let A be the event of 4 Hearts and
3 Diamonds. To form A we have to choose 4 Hearts out of 13, and
133 Diamonds
13 26out of 13,
followed by 6 cards out of 26 Spades and Clubs. Thus, |A| = 4 × 3 × 6 . So that
P (A) = |A|/|Ω| ≈ 0.074.

2.3

Inclusion-Exclusion Principle

The inclusion-exclusion principle is a fundamental concept in probability and combinatorics, used
to calculate the probability of the union of several events. It accounts for the fact that when we
simply add the probabilities of individual events, we may be overcounting the outcomes that occur
in the intersection of these events. In a combinatorics course (e.g. MATH1061/MATH1081 at UQ),
this principle is typically stated in terms of the cardinality of sets. Here we state it in terms of
probability. Note that it applies to any probability space, not just equiprobable ones.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

35

Counting, Inclusion-Exclusion, and Independence

36

Two or Three Events
Ω

C
Ω
A

A∩B

B

A

(a) Two events

B

(b) Three events

Figure 2.6: Venn diagrams for the inclusion-exclusion principle. (a) Two events A and B. (b) Three
events A, B, and C.
For two events A and B (Figure 2.6a), the principle states:
P (A ∪ B) = P (A) + P (B) − P (A ∩ B) .

(2.7)

In words: to find the probability that at least one of A or B occurs, we add their individual probabilities, then subtract the probability of their overlap, which was counted twice.
For three events A, B, and C (Figure 2.6b), the principle extends to:
P (A ∪ B ∪ C) = P (A) + P (B) + P (C) − P (A ∩ B) − P (A ∩ C) − P (B ∩ C) + P (A ∩ B ∩ C) . (2.8)
In words: we first add the three individual probabilities, then subtract the three pairwise overlaps
(each of which was double-counted), then add back the triple overlap (which was subtracted too
many times).
Note that in both cases, if the events are disjoint (i.e., all intersections have probability zero),
the formulas reduce to simply adding the individual probabilities. This is just Axiom 3 (finite
additivity) from Chapter 1.

The General Case: n Events
For n events A1 , A2 , . . . , An , the inclusion-exclusion principle states:

+P (A1 ∩ A2 ∩ A3 ), recovering the three-event formula above.

2.4

Independence of Events

Independence is a very important concept in probability and statistics. Informally, two events are
independent if the occurrence of one has no bearing on the likelihood of the other. The formal
definition requires only the multiplication rule:
A, B independent ⇔ P (A ∩ B) = P (A) P (B)
We will revisit independence using the language of conditional probability in Chapter 4, and in
the context of random variables starting in Chapter 3. We can extend the definition to arbitrarily
many events:
Definition : Independent Events — The events A1 , A2 , . . . , are said to be (mutually) independent

if for any k and any choice of distinct indices i1 , . . . , ik ,

P (Ai1 ∩ Ai2 ∩ · · · ∩ Aik ) = P (Ai1 ) P (Ai2 ) · · · P (Aik ) .

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

(2.20)

Counting, Inclusion-Exclusion, and Independence

40

Independence enters in two ways. First, when constructing a
probability model — for example, modelling repeated trials as independent. Second, as a derived
property that must be proved — for example, it can be shown that the sample mean and sample
variance from a normal sample are independent (see Chapter 10), a result that is far from obvious
and requires careful analysis.

Remark (Independence in Practice).

A fair coin and a fair six-sided die are thrown together. Since the two
objects do not physically affect each other, we expect the coin outcome and the die outcome to be
independent. We construct the probability space in two ways and show they agree.
Example (A coin and a die).

Construction 1 (Equiprobable space). The sample space lists all 2 × 6 = 12 outcomes:
Ω = {H1, H2, H3, H4, H5, H6, T 1, T 2, T 3, T 4, T 5, T 6} .

(2.21)

Since both the coin and the die are fair, every outcome is equally likely, so P ({ω}) = 1/12 for
each ω ∈ Ω. Now let A = {H1, H2, H3, H4, H5, H6} be the event that the coin shows heads, and
B = {H5, H6, T 5, T 6} the event that the die shows 5 or more. Then
P (A) =

1
6
= ,
12 2

P (B) =

4
1
= ,
12 3

P (A ∩ B) = P ({H5, H6}) =

2
1
= .
12 6

(2.22)

Since P (A ∩ B) = 16 = 12 · 13 = P (A) P (B), the events A and B are independent. One can verify that
the same holds for any event defined purely in terms of the coin and any event defined purely in
terms of the die.
Construction 2 (Independence by design). Alternatively, we can build the model from the start
using independence. Define the coin probability space with Ω1 = {H, T } and P 1 ({H}) = P 1 ({T }) =
1/2, and the die probability space with Ω2 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} and P 2 ({k}) = 1/6 for each k. The joint
sample space is the Cartesian product Ω = Ω1 × Ω2 , and we define the joint probability measure by
the product rule:
P ({(c, d)}) = P 1 ({c}) · P 2 ({d}) ,

c ∈ Ω1 , d ∈ Ω2 .
(2.23)
1
1 1
For example, P ({(H, 4)}) = 2 · 6 = 12 . This gives each of the 12 outcomes probability 1/12, exactly
as in Construction 1. Independence of coin events and die events is guaranteed by construction: for
any A1 ⊆ Ω1 and A2 ⊆ Ω2 ,
P (A1 × A2 ) = P 1 (A1 ) · P 2 (A2 ) .

(2.24)

The two constructions yield the same probability space. Construction 1 starts from an equiprobable space and proves independence. Construction 2 starts from separate experiments and builds
independence into the model via the product rule. The second approach generalises naturally to
combining any number of independent experiments.

Independence versus Disjointness
A common misconception is to confuse independent events with disjoint (mutually exclusive)
events. In fact, for events with positive probability, the two notions are opposites.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Counting, Inclusion-Exclusion, and Independence

41

If A and B are disjoint, then A ∩ B = ∅, so P (A ∩ B) = 0. But if both P (A) > 0 and P (B) > 0,
then P (A) P (B) > 0. Therefore
P (A ∩ B) = 0 , P (A) P (B) > 0 ,

(2.25)

which means A and B are not independent. In other words, two events with positive probability
cannot be both disjoint and independent.
Intuitively, disjoint events are as far from independent as possible: knowing that one occurred
tells us the other certainly did not occur. Independent events with positive probability, on the other
hand, always overlap — they must satisfy P (A ∩ B) > 0.
Roll a fair die. Let A = {1, 2} (roll is at most 2) and
B = {5, 6} (roll is at least 5). These events are disjoint: A ∩ B = ∅. We have P (A) = 1/3 and
P (B) = 1/3, but P (A ∩ B) = 0 , 1/9 = P (A) P (B). If we know the roll is 5 or 6, there is zero
chance it is 1 or 2. The events are not independent.

Example (Disjoint but Not Independent).

Pairwise Independence Does Not Imply Mutual Independence
The definition of mutual independence requires the multiplication rule to hold for every subcollection of events, not just for pairs. The following example shows that pairwise independence alone is
not enough.
Toss two fair coins. The sample space is Ω =
{HH, HT, T H, T T }, each with probability 1/4. Define three events:
Example (Pairwise but Not Mutually Independent).

A = {first coin is H} = {HH, HT } ,
B = {second coin is H} = {HH, T H} ,
C = {both coins show the same face} = {HH, T T } .

(2.26)
(2.27)
(2.28)

Each event has probability 1/2. The pairwise intersections are:
A ∩ B = {HH} ,

A ∩ C = {HH} ,

B ∩ C = {HH} ,

(2.29)

each with probability 1/4 = 21 · 12 . So every pair satisfies the multiplication rule: the events are
pairwise independent.
However, A ∩ B ∩ C = {HH} has probability 1/4, while P (A) P (B) P (C) = 21 · 21 · 12 = 81 . Since
1/4 , 1/8, the three events are not mutually independent. Intuitively, knowing that A and B both
occurred (first coin H, second coin H) tells us with certainty that C occurred (both coins match), so
C is not independent of the pair {A, B}.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

3

D ISCRETE R ANDOM VARIABLES , THEIR
D ISTRIBUTIONS , AND M OMENTS

In this chapter we consider the general notion of random variables, focusing on discrete valued
random variables. With this we study their distributions, and moment descriptors, including expectation, mean, and variance. In particular, we study families of random variables associated
with independent Bernoulli (coin-flip) trials. This includes the binomial, geometric, and negative
Binomial distributions. We also visit the Poisson distribution and the hypergeometric distribution.

3.1

Discrete Random Variables and Their Distributions

A random variable is a function from a sample space to the real numbers.
Definition : Random Variable — A random variable is a (measurable) function X : Ω → R. That

is, X assigns a real number X(ω) to each outcome ω ∈ Ω.

Measurability is a technical condition ensuring that the sets below are events (elements of the
σ-algebra); we will not dwell on it in this course.
Although random variables are mathematically functions, it is often helpful to think of a random
variable as a quantity whose value will be revealed once we carry out the experiment. We usually
denote random variables with capital letters from the end of the alphabet, e.g. X, Y, Z.

42

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

43

Since X is a function on Ω, any statement about the values of X defines an event — a subset of
Ω. For a set C ⊆ R, we write
{X ∈ C} := {ω ∈ Ω : X(ω) ∈ C}.

(3.1)

This is the set of all outcomes ω for which the value X(ω) falls in C. Probabilities involving X are
therefore ordinary probabilities of events:

P (X ∈ C) = P {ω ∈ Ω : X(ω) ∈ C} .
(3.2)
Common special cases include {X = x} = {ω : X(ω) = x}, {X ⩽ x} = {ω : X(ω) ⩽ x}, and
{a < X ⩽ b} = {ω : a < X(ω) ⩽ b}. These abbreviations are standard and we use them freely
throughout.
Suppose we toss two fair dice and note their sum. If we observe
each die separately, the sample space is Ω = {(i, j) : i, j ∈ {1, . . . , 6}}, with |Ω| = 36 equally likely
outcomes. The function X defined by X(i, j) = i + j is a random variable mapping each outcome to
the corresponding sum, as depicted in Figure 3.1.
Example (Sum of Two Dice).

All outcomes in the circled set are mapped to 8. This set is precisely the event {X = 8} = {ω ∈
Ω : X(ω) = 8}. Since it contains 5 outcomes, and all outcomes in Ω are equally likely,

Figure 3.1: A random variable representing the sum of two dice. The function X maps each outcome (i, j) ∈ Ω to the sum i + j.
We distinguish between two broad types of random variables:
Discrete random variables can only take isolated values — their range is a finite or countably
infinite subset of R.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

Continuous random variables can take values in an interval (or more generally a union of intervals), and their distribution is described by a probability density function (see Chapter 5).

The description of continuous random variables here is informal; the formal definition via probability density functions is given in Chapter 5. Some random variables are neither purely discrete nor
purely continuous (for example, mixtures of the two types), though we will mostly not encounter
these in this course.
In this chapter we focus on discrete random variables. Continuous random variables are treated
in Chapter 5. Along the way, we also introduce general concepts — such as the cumulative distribution function, expectation, and variance — that apply to both discrete and continuous random
variables.
In this course our random variables take values in R (or a subset of it); from Chapter 7 onwards we also consider random vectors taking values in Rn . However, in more advanced settings
— particularly in machine learning — one often considers probability distributions over very complicated spaces. For example, a generative model for images defines a probability distribution over
the space of all possible colour images. The core ideas of probability mass functions, densities,
and expectations carry over to those settings, but the mathematical framework requires additional
machinery beyond the scope of this course.

The Distribution of a Random Variable
The distribution of a random variable is a somewhat fluid term: it refers to any complete description
of the probabilities of events associated with the random variable. There are several common ways
to specify a distribution:
• The probability mass function (pmf) — for discrete random variables (this chapter).
• The probability density function (pdf) — for continuous random variables (Chapter 5).
• The cumulative distribution function (cdf) — works for both discrete and continuous random variables, and is defined below.
There are also many other ways to characterise a distribution, including the hazard rate function,
probability generating functions, moment generating functions, and characteristic functions (see
Chapter 9), among others. All of these are equivalent in the sense that knowing any one of them
(under mild conditions) determines the full distribution.
Example (Sum of Two Dice — Continued).

Returning to the random variable X = i + j from Ex-

ample 3.1, its pmf is

Probability Mass Function
Let’s now study the pmf and its properties.
Definition : Probability Mass Function — Let X be a discrete random variable with possible values

x1 , x2 , . . . The probability mass function (pmf) of X is the function f defined by
f (x) = P (X = x),

for each x ∈ {x1 , x2 , . . .}.

(3.6)

We sometimes write fX instead of f to stress that the pmf refers to the random variable X.

Formally, we can treat f as a function f : R → [0, 1] by setting f (x) = 0 for x < {x1 , x2 , . . .}.
The set of values where f (x) > 0 is called the support of the distribution.
Note that some authors call f (x) a “density”; in this course we reserve the term density (or probability density function) exclusively for continuous distributions, introduced in Chapter 5. Also,
some texts write p(x) for the probability mass function; we use f (x) throughout for both the discrete and continuous cases.
The pmf satisfies the following two properties:
1. f (x) ⩾ 0 for all x,
X
2.
f (x) = 1 (the sum is over all values in the support of X).
x

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

46

If we know the pmf, we can calculate any probability involving X:
X
P (X ∈ B) =
f (x)

(3.7)

x∈B

for any subset B of the range of X.
Whenever we have a function that satisfies the properties of the pmf, it means there is a probability space (Ω, F , P ), and a random variable X, where the distribution of X implied by the probability
measure P is described by the pmf.

Cumulative Distribution Function
The cdf is often not considered as the first choice for the description of the distribution of a random
variable, yet it has the advantage that it is not limited only to discrete random variables. Note that
in probability some people just call it the “distribution function” without “cumulative”.
Definition : Cumulative Distribution Function — The cumulative distribution function (cdf) of

a random variable X is the function F : R → [0, 1] defined by
F(x) := P (X ⩽ x),

x ∈ R.

(3.8)

Note that the cdf is defined for all real values of x, regardless of what the support of X is.
Claim 5: Basic Properties of the CDF — For any random variable X with cdf F,

1. 0 ⩽ F(x) ⩽ 1 for all x,
2. F is non-decreasing: x ⩽ y ⇒ F(x) ⩽ F(y),
3. lim F(x) = 0 and lim F(x) = 1.
x→−∞

x→∞

Proof:
1. Since F(x) = P (X ⩽ x) is a probability, it lies in [0, 1].
2. If x ⩽ y, then {X ⩽ x} ⊆ {X ⩽ y}, so F(x) = P (X ⩽ x) ⩽ P (X ⩽ y) = F(y) by Claim 2(2).
3. As x → −∞, the events {X ⩽ x} decrease to ∅, so F(x) → P (∅) = 0 by continuity of
probability (Claim 3). As x → ∞, the events {X ⩽ x} increase to Ω, so F(x) → P (Ω) = 1.
□
Claim 6: Probabilities of Half-Open Intervals — For any random variable X with cdf F, and any

a < b,
P (a < X ⩽ b) = F(b) − F(a).

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

(3.9)

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

47

Proof: The event {X ⩽ b} can be written as the disjoint union {X ⩽ a} ∪ {a < X ⩽ b}. By additivity,
P (X ⩽ b) = P (X ⩽ a) + P (a < X ⩽ b),

(3.10)

so P (a < X ⩽ b) = F(b) − F(a).

□

As cdfs will reappear in Chapter 5 with continuous random variables, there it will not matter
whether the inequalities are < or ⩽, but here in the discrete case it does.
Figure 3.3 shows the graph of a cdf of a random variable that has both discrete and continuous
components. Notice the jumps (corresponding to point masses), the smooth sections (corresponding to continuous parts), the right-continuity (closed dots on the right side of each jump), and the
limits at ±∞.
1
F(x)

0
x

Figure 3.3: A cumulative distribution function. The jumps correspond to values with positive
probability; between jumps the function is constant (for a discrete random variable) or continuously
increasing (for a continuous one).
For discrete random variables, F is a step function with jumps at the support points. Note that
the proof of the following claim is not required as part of the material.
Claim 7: Right-Continuity of the CDF — For any random variable X with cdf F, the function F is

A related function is the complementary cumulative distribution function (ccdf), defined by
F̄(x) = 1 − F(x) = P (X > x). It is also called the survival function, especially in reliability theory
and survival analysis.

PMF ↔ CDF Conversions for Discrete Distributions
For discrete random variables, given one of the pmf or cdf we can always recover the other (see
Figure 3.4 below for an illustration). Hence both the cdf and pmf are alternative ways to describe
the distribution of the random variable.
PMF to CDF. For a discrete random variable,
F(x) =

X

f (k).

(3.14)

k⩽x

Note that the summation is restricted to the support points of f that are smaller or equal to x.
CDF to PMF. The pmf at a point x equals the jump size of F at x:
f (x) = F(x) − F(x− ),

(3.15)

where F(x− ) = limh↓0 F(x − h) denotes the left-hand limit of F at x. That is, F(x− ) is the value of
the cdf just before the point x, and F(x) is the value at x itself. The difference is exactly the size
of the jump at x, which equals P (X = x) = f (x). If x is not a support point, there is no jump, and
f (x) = 0.

3.2

Moments: Expectation, Mean, and Variance

Now that we can describe the distribution of a discrete random variable via its pmf or cdf, we turn
to numerical summaries that capture key features of the distribution. The most fundamental of
these are the expectation (a measure of centre) and the variance (a measure of spread).

Expectation (Mean)
The expectation (or mean) of a random variable is a weighted average of its possible values,
weighted by their probabilities. It represents the “centre of mass” of the distribution.
Definition : Expectation (Mean) — The expectation (or mean) of a discrete random variable X with

pmf f is
E[X] =

X

x f (x),

(3.23)

x

P
provided the sum converges absolutely ( x |x| f (x) < ∞). We also write µ = E[X] or µX = E[X].

The expectation can be thought of as the long-run average: if we repeat the experiment many
times and record the values of X, the sample average will converge to E[X]. This is made precise
by the Law of Large Numbers (Chapter 10). Even if we only get to observe a single realisation of
X, the expectation describes a “typical value”. Note, however, that this typical value may not be
one of the possible values of X, as we now illustrate.
Consider the random variable X from Example 3.1, which takes values 0, 1, 2 with probabilities
0.3, 0.5, 0.2. Its mean is
E[X] = 0 · 0.3 + 1 · 0.5 + 2 · 0.2 = 0 + 0.5 + 0.4 = 0.9.
Example (Tossing a Fair Die).

be a value that X can actually take.

The expectation as a centre of mass

The expectation has a physical interpretation as a centre of mass. Imagine that point masses with
weights p1 , p2 , . . . , pn are placed at positions x1 , x2 , . . . , xn on the real line (Figure 3.6). The centre
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

52

centre of mass = x1 p1 + x2 p2 + · · · + xn pn ,

(3.26)

which is exactly E[X] for a discrete random variable X taking values x1 , . . . , xn with probabilities
p1 , . . . , pn .

Figure 3.6: The expectation as a centre of mass. Point masses p1 , . . . , pn are placed at positions

An immediate consequence is that if the pmf is symmetric about a point c — that is, f (c + y) =
f (c − y) for all y — then E[X] = c (when the expectation exists).

Expectation of a Function of a Random Variable
We often need to compute the expectation of some function h(X) of a random variable X. One
approach is to first derive the pmf of the new random variable Y = h(X) and then compute E[Y]
directly. We consider transformations of random variables in Chapter 6. However, there is a much
more convenient shortcut.
Claim 8: Law of the Unconscious Statistician (LOTUS) — If X is a discrete random variable with

pmf f and h : R → R is any function, then
E[h(X)] =

X

h(x) f (x),

(3.27)

x

provided the sum converges absolutely.

The name reflects the fact that one can compute E[h(X)] “unconsciously” — without first deriving the distribution of h(X).
For the random variable X from Example 3.1, taking h(x) = x2 gives
E[X 2 ] = 02 · 0.3 + 12 · 0.5 + 22 · 0.2 = 0 + 0.5 + 0.8 = 1.3.

(3.28)

Why does LOTUS work? (Informal argument.) Recall that X is a function from the sample
space Ω to R, mapping each outcome ω to a value X(ω). When we define Y = h(X), we are
composing two functions: first ω 7→ X(ω), then X(ω) 7→ h(X(ω)).

Moments
An important family of functions arises by taking h(x) = xk for non-negative integers k, or e
h(x) =
(x − µ)k where µ = E[X], as used for central moments.
Definition : Moments — The k-th moment of a random variable X is

E[X k ] =

X

xk f (x),

k = 0, 1, 2, . . .

(3.31)

x

The k-th central moment is

 X

(x − µ)k f (x),

where µ = E[X].

(3.32)

x

The zeroth moment is always E[X 0 ] = 1. The first moment E[X] is the mean. The second
central moment E[(X − µ)2 ] is the variance, which we define next.
Moments are important for two reasons. First, the most commonly used summary quantities
— the mean and the variance — are themselves moments/central-moments. Second, the infinite
sequence of moments often characterises the distribution entirely: under mild conditions, if two
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

54

distributions share all their moments, they must be the same distribution. Keep in mind, however, that knowing only a few moments does not determine the distribution exactly, but the more
moments we know, the more information we have about it.

Variance and Standard Deviation
The mean tells us where the distribution is centred, but says nothing about how spread out the
values are. The variance measures this spread.
Definition : Variance and Standard Deviation — The variance of a random variable X with mean

µ = E[X] is

 X
Var(X) = E (X − µ)2 =
(x − µ)2 f (x).

(3.33)

x

The standard deviation is SD(X) =

√
Var(X).

The variance is the expected squared deviation from the mean — it is always non-negative. The
standard deviation has the same units as X and is often more interpretable.
For the random variable X from Example 3.1 with µ = 0.9,
Var(X) = (0 − 0.9)2 · 0.3 + (1 − 0.9)2 · 0.5 + (2 − 0.9)2 · 0.2 = 0.243 + 0.005 + 0.242 = 0.49, (3.34)
√
and SD(X) = 0.49 = 0.7.

X 7→ aX + b.

Sequences of Random Variables

So far we have considered a single random variable X defined on a sample space Ω. But many
situations involve several random variables, all defined on the same underlying experiment.

Multiple Random Variables on the Same Sample Space
Since a random variable is simply a function X : Ω → R, nothing prevents us from defining two,
three, or more such functions on the same sample space. For instance, if we roll two dice so that
Ω = {(i, j) : i, j ∈ {1, . . . , 6}}, we might define X1 (i, j) = i (the first die) and X2 (i, j) = j (the second
die). Both X1 and X2 are random variables on Ω.
More generally, we can consider a sequence of random variables X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn (or even an
infinite sequence X1 , X2 , X3 , . . .), all defined as functions on the same sample space Ω. Each Xi has
its own pmf, mean, and variance, but the random variables may also be related to one another —
knowing the value of X1 might give us information about X2 .

Independence of Random Variables
In Chapter 2 we defined independence of events. We now extend this to random variables.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

59

Definition : Independent Random Variables — Two discrete random variables X and Y are inde-

pendent if for all values x and y,
P (X = x, Y = y) = P (X = x) P (Y = y).

(3.69)

More generally, random variables X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn are mutually independent if
P (X1 = x1 , X2 = x2 , . . . , Xn = xn ) = P (X1 = x1 ) P (X2 = x2 ) · · · P (Xn = xn )

(3.70)

for all values x1 , x2 , . . . , xn .

Independence means that knowing the value of any subset of the random variables gives no
information about the others. In our two-dice example, X1 and X2 are independent: the outcome of
the first die tells us nothing about the second.
The probability P (X = x, Y = y) is called the joint probability
mass function of X and Y. In Chapter 7 we will study joint distributions in detail, providing a
richer framework for analysing the relationship between multiple random variables. For now, the
definition above is all we need.
Remark (Joint distributions).

IID Sequences
An especially important special case arises when we repeat the same experiment independently
many times.
Definition : IID Sequence —

A sequence of random variables X1 , X2 , . . . is independent and

identically distributed (iid) if:
1. the random variables are mutually independent, and
2. they all have the same probability distribution (the same pmf, the same mean, the same variance,
etc.).
iid

We write X1 , X2 , . . . ∼ F to indicate that each Xi has distribution F and the sequence is iid.

The iid assumption is one of the most common modelling assumptions in probability and statistics. It captures the idea of repeatable, non-interacting experiments: each trial is a fresh copy of
the same random mechanism, unaffected by what happened before.
Suppose we roll a fair die repeatedly. Let Xi be the outcome of the
i-th roll. Then X1 , X2 , X3 , . . . is an iid sequence, with each Xi ∼ DU({1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}). Knowledge of
X1 , . . . , Xk gives no information about Xk+1 — each roll is independent and has the same uniform
distribution.
Example (IID Dice Throws).

The following code simulates 15 iid rolls of a fair die.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments
Simulating an iid sequence of dice throws

LLM Prompt > Write Python code that simulates and prints 15 iid rolls of a fair six-sided die.

IID dice rolls:

[2 3 1 6 6 6 5 5 4 6 5 5 3 6 4]

Each roll is independent of all others and has the same distribution, so this is an iid sequence.
Many of the distributions in the next two sections — binomial, geometric, negative binomial — arise from counting patterns in an iid sequence of Bernoulli random
variables. The iid assumption is what makes these counts tractable.

Remark (Looking ahead).

In Chapter 4 we will encounter Markov chains, which are sequences of random variables that
are not iid: the distribution of Xn+1 depends on the current value Xn . The contrast between iid
sequences and Markov chains is a central theme in probability.

3.4

Bernoulli Trials and Related Distributions

A Bernoulli trial is an experiment with exactly two outcomes, conventionally called success and
failure. We denote the probability of success by p ∈ [0, 1] and the probability of failure by q = 1− p.
A sequence of independent Bernoulli trials consists of repeated independent performances of
such an experiment, each with the same success probability p.
Many important discrete distributions arise from an iid sequence of Bernoulli random variables.
Specifically, let X1 , X2 , X3 , . . . be an iid sequence with each Xi ∼ Ber(p) (as defined below). Each
remaining distribution in this section — binomial, geometric, and negative binomial — is obtained
as a function (mapping) of this iid sequence:
• The binomial counts the total number of successes in the first n trials: S n = X1 + · · · + Xn .
• The geometric records the index of the first success: T = min{i ⩾ 1 : Xi = 1}.
• The negative binomial records the index of the r-th success: T r = min{i : X1 + · · · + Xi = r}.
Thus all three are simply different ways of summarising the same underlying iid Bernoulli sequence.

Bernoulli Distribution
The simplest random variable associated with a Bernoulli trial records whether the trial resulted in
success or failure.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Definition : Bernoulli Distribution — A random variable X has a Bernoulli distribution with parameter p ∈ [0, 1], written X ∼ Ber(p), if X takes the value 1 (success) with probability p and 0 (failure)

with probability q = 1 − p. The pmf is
f (x) = p x (1 − p)1−x ,

x ∈ {0, 1}.

(3.71)

Mean.
E[X] = 0 · (1 − p) + 1 · p = p.

(3.73)

Second moment. Since X takes values 0 and 1 only, X 2 = X, so E[X 2 ] = E[X] = p.
Variance.

By the computational formula for variance,
Var(X) = E[X 2 ] − (E[X])2 = p − p2 = p(1 − p).

(3.74)

In summary,
X ∼ Ber(p) :
E[X] = p,
Var(X) = p(1 − p).
(3.75)
Note that the variance is maximised at p = 1/2 (where Var(X) = 1/4) and equals zero at p = 0 and
p = 1 (where there is no randomness).
The cdf of the Bernoulli distribution is a step function with a single jump at x = 0 of size 1 − p
and a second jump at x = 1 of size p (Figure 3.8).

Negative Binomial Distribution
The geometric distribution counts the number of trials until the first success. The negative binomial
distribution generalises this to the r-th success.
Definition : Negative Binomial Distribution — Let X be the number of trials until the r-th success in a sequence of independent Ber(p) trials. Then X has a negative binomial distribution with
parameters r and p, written X ∼ NegBin(r, p). The support is {r, r + 1, r + 2, . . .} and the pmf is

!
x−1 r
f (x) =
p (1 − p) x−r ,
r−1

x = r, r + 1, r + 2, . . .

(3.97)

To see why, consider the event {X = x}: the x-th trial must be a success
 x−1 (and the r-th overall), so
among the first x−1 trials there are exactly r −1 successes. The factor r−1 counts the arrangements
of those r − 1 successes among x − 1 trials, pr is the probability of the r successes, and (1 − p) x−r
is the probability of the x − r failures.
Note that NegBin(1, p) = Geom(p).
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

67

The mean and variance can be computed in several ways which we do not show here. One
approach is to recognise that X is a sum of r independent Geom(p) random variables (one for each
successive success) and use properties of sums of independent random variables. Another approach
uses probability generating functions (transforms). The result is intuitive: if on average it takes 1/p
trials to get one success, it should take r/p trials to get r successes.
In summary,
X ∼ NegBin(r, p), X ∈ {r, r + 1, . . .} :

E[X] =

3.5

Poisson and Hypergeometric Distributions

In this section we introduce two further discrete distributions: the Poisson distribution, which models the count of events during a time interval, and the hypergeometric distribution, which models
sampling without replacement. Both are closely related to the binomial distribution, as we establish
at the end of the section.

Poisson Distribution
The Poisson distribution arises naturally as a model for the number of events occurring in a fixed
interval of time or space, when events happen independently at a constant average rate. Typical
examples include the number of emails received per hour, the number of typos on a page, or the
number of radioactive decays per second.
Definition : Poisson Distribution — A random variable X has a Poisson distribution with parameter λ > 0, written X ∼ Poi(λ), if

f (x) =

e−λ λ x

x = 0, 1, 2, . . .

Hypergeometric Distribution
The hypergeometric distribution arises when sampling without replacement from a finite population of two types. A typical scenario: an urn contains N balls, of which K are red and N − K are
blue. We draw n balls without replacement and count the number of red balls drawn.

Relations to the Binomial Distribution
Both the Poisson and hypergeometric distributions are intimately connected to the binomial distribution through limiting arguments.
Poisson limit of the binomial

When the number of trials n is large and the success probability p is small, with the product np ≈ λ
held roughly constant, the binomial distribution is well approximated by a Poisson distribution.

This result is sometimes called the “law of rare events”: when many independent trials each

Intuitively, when the population is vast compared to the sample, removing a few items barely
changes the composition of the remaining population, so each draw is nearly independent with the
same success probability p = K/N. The finite population correction (N − n)/(N − 1) → 1 in this
regime, confirming that the variance also converges to np(1 − p).
A standard rule of thumb is that the binomial
approximation to the hypergeometric is adequate when n/N ⩽ 0.05 (the sample is at most 5%
of the population). For the quality control example (Example 3.5), n/N = 10/50 = 0.2, so the
correction matters. But if the shipment had N = 5000 components with K = 500 defective and we
still sampled n = 10, then n/N = 0.002 and Bin(10, 0.1) would be an excellent approximation.
Example (When to use the binomial approximation).

3.6

Simulation of Discrete Random Variables

We have met several families of discrete distributions — Bernoulli, binomial, geometric, negative binomial, Poisson, and hypergeometric. A natural question is: how can we generate random
samples from a given distribution on a computer? We have already seen some answers for specific
cases. Here we develop a general method.

Specific Algorithms for Specific Distributions
For each of the distributions studied so far, there is a natural simulation strategy based on the
definition of the distribution:
• Bernoulli. Draw U ∼ U(0, 1) and set X = 1 if U < p, else X = 0 (see Section 3.4).
P
• Binomial. Sum n independent Bernoulli trials: X = ni=1 Ii where each Ii ∼ Ber(p).
• Geometric. Repeat independent Ber(p) trials until the first success; count the trial number.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

74

• Negative Binomial. Repeat independent Ber(p) trials until the r-th success; count failures
(Example 3.4).
• Discrete Uniform on {a, a + 1, . . . , b}: draw a uniform integer from this range.
For the discrete uniform case, Python provides a direct function:
Simulating a discrete uniform random variable

LLM Prompt > Show how to generate a single draw from a discrete uniform distribution on {3, 4, . . . , 8}

in Python using NumPy.

There are also specialised algorithms for other distributions (e.g. efficient methods for the Poisson distribution exist, but we will not cover them here). The question is: is there a single method
that works for any discrete distribution?

The Cumulative Probability Method
P
Suppose X takes values x1 , x2 , x3 , . . . with probabilities p1 , p2 , p3 , . . . (where k pk = 1). The idea
is to partition the interval [0, 1) into consecutive sub-intervals of lengths p1 , p2 , p3 , . . .:
[0, p1 ) [p1 , p1 + p2 ) [p1 + p2 , p1 + p2 + p3 ) · · ·
| {z } | {z } |
{z
}
x1

x2

(3.120)

x3

Now draw U ∼ U(0, 1): whichever sub-interval U falls in determines X. Since U is uniform on
[0, 1), the probability that U falls in the k-th sub-interval is exactly pk , as required.
Concretely, set X = xk where k is the smallest index such that
p1 + p2 + · · · + pk > U.

(3.121)

This gives the following algorithm.
Algorithm 3.6.1: Cumulative Probability Method for a Discrete Distribution
1. Generate U ∼ U(0, 1).
2. Set S = 0, k = 1.
3. While S ⩽ U: set S = S + pk , k = k + 1.
4. Return X = xk−1 .
This method works whether the support is finite or infinite: if the support is infinite the while
loop in Step 3 will always terminate in finitely many steps (with probability 1), since the cumulative
sum converges to 1.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Discrete Random Variables, their Distributions, and Moments

75

Algorithm 3.6.1 is a special case of the inversetransform method (also called the inverse probability transform), which provides a general technique for simulating from any distribution — discrete or continuous. We will study this method
formally in Section 6.2.

Remark (Preview of the inverse-transform method).

CHAPTER

4

C ONDITIONAL P ROBABILITY AND M ARKOV
C HAINS

In this chapter we introduce basics of conditional probability. With this we visit the law of total
probability and Bayes’ theorem. We also revisit some aspects of discrete random variables from
the previous chapter, now through the lens of conditional probability. We also visit the product
rule and then introduce the concept of Markov chains. These stochastic processes are a sequence
of discrete random variables that exhibit the Markov property. In their elementary study, we see
how matrix multiplication is inherently related to their probability laws, and we visit basic concepts
such as their limiting distribution at an introductory exploratory level.

4.1

Conditional Probability

How do probabilities change when we know some event B ⊆ Ω has occurred? Suppose B has
occurred. Thus, we know that the outcome lies in B. Then A will occur if and only if A ∩ B occurs,
and the relative chance of A occurring is therefore P (A ∩ B)/P (B).

In Section 1.3 we introduced Kolmogorov’s axioms and the properties that every probability measure must satisfy. A natural question is: does the conditional probability P (· | B), viewed as a function
of events, itself define a valid probability measure?

Since P (· | B) satisfies the axioms, all properties derived from them in Section 1.3 — the complement rule, monotonicity, inclusion–exclusion, and so on — automatically hold for conditional
probabilities as well. In other words, (Ω, F , P (· | B)) is a valid probability triple, and we may work
with conditional probabilities using exactly the same tools we developed in Chapter 1.

Constructing a Probability Model via Conditional Probability
In the dice example above we started with a fully specified probability space and then derived
conditional probabilities from it. In practice, models are often built the other way around: we
specify conditional probabilities as part of the model and use them to determine the joint probability
of all events.
