Independence and Conditional Probability
In Chapter 2 we defined events A and B to be independent if P (A ∩ B) = P (A) P (B). Note that this
definition is symmetric: A independent of B is the same as B independent of A. With the language
of conditional probability, we can now see what this really means. If A and B are independent and
P (B) > 0, then
P (A ∩ B) P (A) P (B)
=
= P (A).
(4.9)
P (A | B) =
P (B)
P (B)
Similarly, if P (A) > 0, then P (B | A) = P (B). In other words, independence says precisely that
conditioning on one event does not change the probability of the other. Knowing that B has occurred
gives no new information about whether A occurs, and vice versa. Conversely, if P (A | B) = P (A),
then P (A ∩ B) = P (A) P (B), recovering the definition from Chapter 2.
In the dice example above, the events “sum is 10” and “one 6 is cast” are not independent:
conditioning on the sum changed the probability from 10/36 to 2/3.
Roll two fair dice. Let A = {first die shows 6} and
B = {second die shows 1}. Then P (A) = 1/6, P (B) = 1/6, and A ∩ B = {(6, 1)} with P (A ∩ B) =
1/36 = P (A) P (B). So A and B are independent, and indeed P (A | B) = P (A) = 1/6 — knowing the
second die shows 1 tells us nothing about the first die.
Example (Independent Events with Two Dice).

Conditional Independence
Two events A and B may be independent unconditionally, yet become dependent once we condition
on a third event C — or vice versa. This motivates the following definition.
Definition : Conditional Independence — Events A and B are said to be conditionally independ-

ent given C (with P (C) > 0) if
P (A ∩ B | C) = P (A | C) P (B | C).

(4.10)

Equivalently, by the results of the previous subsection, conditional independence means
P (A | B ∩ C) = P (A | C) whenever P (B ∩ C) > 0: learning that B occurred provides no additional
information about A beyond what C already tells us.
It is important to note that (unconditional) independence does not imply conditional independence, nor does conditional independence imply unconditional independence.

Product Rule
By the definition of conditional probability we have
P (A ∩ B) = P (A) P (B | A).

(4.16)

We can generalise this to n intersections A1 ∩ A2 ∩ · · · ∩ An , which we abbreviate as A1 A2 · · · An .
This gives the product rule of probability.
Claim 17: Product Rule — Let A1 , . . . , An be a sequence of events with

P (A1 . . . An−1 ) > 0. Then,

P (A1 · · · An ) = P (A1 ) P (A2 | A1 ) P (A3 | A1 A2 ) · · · P (An | A1 · · · An−1 ).

(4.17)

Proof: We show the proof for 4 events A, B, C, D; the general case follows similarly. By applying
(4.1) to each conditional probability on the right-hand side of (4.17), we have
P (A) P (B | A) P (C | AB) P (D | ABC) = P (A)

P (AB) P (ABC) P (ABCD)
= P (ABCD),

where the last equality follows by cancellation of common factors. The proof for general n follows
the same telescoping argument.

4.2

Law of Total Probability and Bayes’ Theorem

Conditional probability becomes especially powerful when combined with a decomposition of the
sample space into cases. This leads to two fundamental tools: the law of total probability, which
computes unconditional probabilities by averaging over cases, and Bayes’ theorem, which reverses
the direction of conditioning.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

83

Law of Total Probability
Suppose B1 , B2 , . . . , Bn is a partition of Ω. That is, B1 , B2 , . . . , Bn are disjoint and their union is Ω,
see Figure 4.4.

B1

B2

B3

B4

B5

B6

A

Ω
Figure 4.4: A partition of the sample space
Claim 18: Law of Total Probability — Let B1 , . . . , Bn be a partition of Ω with P (Bi ) > 0 for each i,

and let A be any event. Then,
P (A) =

n
X

P (A | Bi ) P (Bi ).

(4.23)

i=1

Proof: Since A = A ∩ Ω = A ∩ (B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bn ) = (A ∩ B1 ) ∪ · · · ∪ (A ∩ Bn ) and the sets A ∩ Bi
P
are disjoint, the sum rule gives P (A) = ni=1 P (A ∩ Bi ). Applying the definition of conditional
probability to each term, P (A ∩ Bi ) = P (A | Bi ) P (Bi ), gives (4.23).
□
Note that if P (Bi ) = 0 for some i, the conditional probability P (A | Bi ) is not defined. However,
the corresponding term P (A | Bi ) P (Bi ) = P (A ∩ Bi ) = 0 contributes nothing to the sum (since
A ∩ Bi ⊆ Bi and P (Bi ) = 0). We may therefore simply omit such terms, or equivalently adopt the
convention that 0 · undefined = 0 in this context.
A company has three factories (1, 2 and 3) that produce the same chip,
producing 15%, 35% and 50% of the total production, respectively. The probability of a defective
chip at 1, 2, 3 is 0.01, 0.05, 0.02, respectively. What is the overall probability that a randomly
selected chip is defective?
Example (Three Factories).

Let Bi denote the event that the chip is produced by factory i. The {Bi } form a partition of Ω.
Let A denote the event that the chip is defective. By the law of total probability,
P (A) = 0.15 × 0.01 + 0.35 × 0.05 + 0.5 × 0.02 = 0.0015 + 0.0175 + 0.01 = 0.029.

(4.24)

So about 2.9% of all chips are defective.

Bayes’ Theorem
Combining the law of total probability with the definition of conditional probability gives Bayes’
theorem:

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

84

Claim 19: Bayes’ Theorem — Let B1 , . . . , Bn be a partition of Ω with P (B j ) > 0 for each j, and let A

be an event with P (A) > 0. Then,

P (A | B j ) P (B j )
P (B j | A) = Pn
.
i=1 P (A | Bi ) P (Bi )

(4.25)

Proof: By the definition of conditional probability, P (B j | A) = P (A ∩ B j )/P (A). The numerator
satisfies P (A ∩ B j ) = P (A | B j ) P (B j ), again by the definition of conditional probability. Replacing
the denominator P (A) by the law of total probability gives (4.25).
□
The power of Bayes’ theorem lies in “reversing the conditioning”: given information about
P (A | B j ) (how likely we are to observe A under each scenario B j ), we can compute P (B j | A) (which
scenario is most likely, given that we observed A).
A transmitter sends a binary digit (0 or 1) over a noisy channel.
The digit sent is 1 with probability P (B1 ) = 0.5 and 0 with probability P (B0 ) = 0.5. The channel
occasionally flips a bit: if a 1 is sent, it is received correctly with probability P (A1 | B1 ) = 0.9
(and received as 0 with probability 0.1). If a 0 is sent, it is received correctly with probability
P (A0 | B0 ) = 0.95 (and received as 1 with probability 0.05).
Example (Noisy Binary Channel).

Suppose we receive a 1. What is the probability that a 1 was actually sent?
By Bayes’ theorem,
0.9 × 0.5
0.45
P (A1 | B1 ) P (B1 )
=
=
≈ 0.947.
P (A1 | B1 ) P (B1 ) + P (A1 | B0 ) P (B0 ) 0.9 × 0.5 + 0.05 × 0.5 0.475
(4.26)
So if we receive a 1, there is about a 94.7% chance that a 1 was indeed sent. The small but nonzero
probability of error (≈ 5.3%) comes from the possibility that a 0 was sent but flipped to 1 by the
channel noise.

The Monty Hall problem is a famous example illustrating how
probabilistic reasoning may sometimes yield surprising results. Consider a contestant on a game
show, with three doors in front of her. One of the doors contains a prize, while the other two are
empty. The contestant is asked to guess which door contains the prize, and she makes a random
guess. Following this, the game show host (GSH) reveals an empty (losing) door from one of
the two remaining doors not chosen. The contestant is then asked if she wishes to stay with her
original choice, or if she wishes to switch to the remaining closed door. The question is: should the
contestant stay with her original choice, or switch? Or perhaps it doesn’t matter.
Example (Monty Hall Problem).

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

B

A

85

C

Figure 4.5: The Monty Hall problem: the prize is behind one of three doors.
The two possible policies (or strategies of play) for the contestant are as follows:
Policy I — Stay with the original choice after the door is revealed.
Policy II — Switch after the door is revealed.
If the player adopts Policy I then she always stays with her initial guess regardless of the GSH’s
action. In this case, her chance of success is 1/3, that is, she wins if her initial choice is correct.
However, if she adopts Policy II then she always switches after the GSH reveals an empty door.
In this case her chance of success is 2/3, that is, she actually wins if her initial guess is incorrect.
This is because the GSH must always reveal a losing door. If she originally chose a losing door,
then the GSH must reveal the second losing door every time (otherwise he would reveal the prize).
That is, if the player chooses an incorrect door at the start, the non-revealed door will always be the
winning door. The chance of such an event is 2/3.
As a further aid for understanding, imagine a case of 100 doors and a single prize behind one
of them. The player chooses a door, say door 1, and following this the GSH reveals 98 losing
doors. There are now only two doors remaining, her choice door 1, and (say for example) door 38.
The intuition of the problem suddenly becomes obvious. The player’s original guess was random
and hence door 1 had a 1/100 chance of containing the prize; however, the GSH’s actions were
constrained — he had to reveal only losing doors, and hence there is a 99/100 chance that door 38
contains the prize. Hence, Policy II is clearly superior.
We now analyse the case of 3 doors by applying Bayes’ theorem. Let Ai be the event that the
prize is behind door i. Let Bi be the event that door i is revealed by the GSH. Then, for example, if

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

86

the player initially chooses door 1 and then the GSH reveals door 2, we have the following:

In the second case note that P (B2 | A3 ) = 1 because the GSH must reveal door 2 if the prize is
behind door 3 since door 1 was already picked. Hence, we see that while neither policy guarantees
a win, Policy II clearly dominates Policy I.

Discrete Random Variables Revisited with Conditioning

In Chapter 3 we studied discrete random variables and their distributions. Now that we have the
tools of conditional probability, we can revisit some of these distributions.

Conditioning on Events Involving Random Variables
We begin with a simple example. Let X be the outcome of rolling a fair die, so X ∼
DU({1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}). Suppose we are told that X ⩾ 3. What is the conditional probability that
X = k?
Using the definition of conditional probability,

The Memoryless Property of the Geometric Distribution
Recall from Chapter 3 that if X ∼ Geom(p), then X counts the number of independent Ber(p)
trials until the first success, with pmf f (k) = (1 − p)k−1 p for k = 1, 2, 3, . . .. We first derive the
complementary cdf (ccdf), P (X > k), and then use it to establish the memoryless property.

Probability argument. The event {X > k} means that the first success has not occurred in the

each with failure probability 1 − p,
P (X > k) = (1 − p)k .

Memoryless property

Suppose we have already performed s trials without a success. What is the probability that we need
more than t additional trials? In other words, what is P (X > s + t | X > s)?
By the definition of conditional probability,
P (X > s + t and X > s)
.
P (X > s)
Since {X > s + t} ⊆ {X > s}, the intersection is simply {X > s + t}. Using the ccdf,
(1 − p) s+t
P (X > s + t | X > s) =
= (1 − p)t = P (X > t).
(1 − p) s
P (X > s + t | X > s) =

(4.35)

(4.36)

Claim 20: Memoryless Property — If X ∼ Geom(p), then for all s, t ⩾ 0,

P (X > s + t | X > s) = P (X > t).

(4.37)

The past — the fact that we have already waited s trials without success — is irrelevant to the
future. The probability of needing more than t additional trials is the same as if we were starting
from scratch. This is the memoryless property: the geometric distribution “forgets” how long it
has been waiting.
Intuitively, this makes sense: each Bernoulli trial is independent, so the coin does not “remember” its previous outcomes. If we have had 100 failures in a row, the chance of success on the next
trial is still p, exactly as on the first trial.
The geometric distribution is the only discrete distribution with the memoryless property. Its
continuous counterpart is the exponential distribution (Chapter 5), which is the only continuous
memoryless distribution.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

4.4

88

Markov Chains Introduction

Markov chains are very important tools for modelling random phenomena. In this chapter we give
a brief introduction to these processes, laying the groundwork for more advanced treatments such
as STAT3004.

A First Example: Beyond IID Sequences
In Chapter 3 we studied iid sequences, where each random variable is independent of all the others. Many real-world phenomena, however, exhibit dependence: the current state influences what
happens next. Markov chains provide the simplest and most important model for such dependent
sequences.
Consider a sequence X0 , X1 , X2 , . . . taking
values in {0, 1}, where the next state depends on the current state via the rule:
Example (Two-State Markov Chain vs. IID Bernoulli).

P (Xn+1 = 0 | Xn = 0) = 0.9,

P (Xn+1 = 1 | Xn = 1) = 0.7.

(4.38)

In other words, there is a high probability of staying in the current state and a small probability
of switching. This is a two-state Markov chain. In contrast, an iid Ber(0.5) sequence has no
memory: each value is equally likely to be 0 or 1, regardless of what came before.
The following code simulates both sequences.
Two-state Markov chain vs. iid Bernoulli

LLM Prompt > Write Python code that simulates and plots (a) an iid Bernoulli(0.5) sequence and (b)

a two-state Markov chain with staying probabilities p00 = 0.9 and p11 = 0.7, both of length 200.
Display them as scatter plots stacked vertically to contrast the iid case with the persistent Markov
chain.

Figure 4.6: An iid Bernoulli sequence (top) versus a two-state Markov chain (bottom). Both take
values in {0, 1}, but the Markov chain exhibits long runs in each state due to the dependence between
consecutive values.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

89

The difference is striking. The iid sequence switches between 0 and 1 frequently and erratically.
The Markov chain, by contrast, tends to stay in the same state for long stretches before switching
— it exhibits persistence.

The Markov Property
The key property of the two-state chain in Example 4.4 is that the conditional distribution of Xn+1 ,
given the entire history X0 , X1 , . . . , Xn , depends only on Xn :
P (Xn+1 = j | X0 = x0 , . . . , Xn = i) = P (Xn+1 = j | Xn = i).

(4.39)

This is called the Markov property: the future is conditionally independent of the past, given the
present. Only the current state matters for predicting the next step.

Computing Distributions via the Product Rule
Knowing the transition probabilities and the starting state, we can compute the distribution of Xn
for any n by repeated application of the law of total probability. Let us see how this works for the
two-state chain of Example 4.4.
The transition probabilities are:
P (Xn+1 = 0 | Xn = 0) = 0.9,
P (Xn+1 = 0 | Xn = 1) = 0.3,

P (Xn+1 = 1 | Xn = 0) = 0.1,
P (Xn+1 = 1 | Xn = 1) = 0.7.

Suppose the chain starts in state 0 with certainty: P (X0 = 0) = 1.
Distribution of X1 .

By the law of total probability, conditioning on X0 :

P (X1 = 0) = P (X1 = 0 | X0 = 0) P (X0 = 0) + P (X1 = 0 | X0 = 1) P (X0 = 1)
= 0.9 × 1 + 0.3 × 0 = 0.9,

(4.40)
(4.41)

P (X1 = 1) = 1 − 0.9 = 0.1.

(4.42)

After one step the chain is still very likely in state 0, since it started there and has a high probability
of staying.
Distribution of X2 . We apply the same idea, now conditioning on X1 . By the Markov property,
the transition probabilities from X1 to X2 are exactly the same as from X0 to X1 :
P (X2 = 0) = P (X2 = 0 | X1 = 0) P (X1 = 0) + P (X2 = 0 | X1 = 1) P (X1 = 1)
= 0.9 × 0.9 + 0.3 × 0.1 = 0.81 + 0.03 = 0.84,

(4.43)
(4.44)

P (X2 = 1) = 1 − 0.84 = 0.16.

(4.45)

Distribution of X3 .

Repeating once more:

P (X3 = 0) = 0.9 × 0.84 + 0.3 × 0.16 = 0.756 + 0.048 = 0.804,

(4.46)

P (X3 = 1) = 1 − 0.804 = 0.196.

(4.47)

We see a clear pattern: the probability of being in state 0 is gradually decreasing from 1, while the
probability of being in state 1 is increasing. The chain is “drifting” towards an equilibrium. One can
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

90

show (and we will, using matrices) that in the long run P (Xn = 0) → 0.75 and P (Xn = 1) → 0.25,
regardless of where the chain started. The key observation is that at each step, we only needed the
distribution of the current state and the transition probabilities to compute the distribution at the
next step. This is a direct consequence of the Markov property.

More Than Two States
The two-state chain above illustrates the key ideas, but Markov chains can have any finite number
of states, and even countably infinitely many. The set of all possible states is called the state space
and we denote it by S. For a finite state space we typically write S = {1, . . . , N} for some finite N;
for a countably infinite state space we write S = {0, 1, 2, . . .}. The Markov property and the law of
total probability calculations carry over in exactly the same way regardless of the size of S.
Consider 6 stones in a pond, labelled 1, . . . , 6, connected by directed
arrows as shown in Figure 4.7. The state space is S = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}. From the current stone,
the next stone is chosen uniformly at random among those reachable by an outgoing arrow. For
example, from stone 5 one can step to stone 4 or stone 6, each with probability 1/2.

Example (Stepping Stones).

3
6

2

Start

5

Figure 4.7: Stepping stones: a directed graph on 6 states.
Let Xn denote the position after n steps, with X0 = 1. This is a Markov chain: given the current
stone Xn , the next stone Xn+1 is determined by the outgoing arrows from Xn alone, regardless of
the path taken to get there. Where would the walker be after 10 steps? What is the probability of
returning to stone 1?
With more than two states, listing all the transition probabilities individually becomes cumbersome. Instead, we arrange them in an array. We can collect all one-step transition probabilities

Another convenient way to describe a Markov chain X is through its transition graph. States
are indicated by the nodes of the graph, and a strictly positive (> 0) transition probability pi j from
state i to j is indicated by an arrow from i to j with weight pi j . For the stepping stone example,
the transition graph would give the graph of Figure 4.7 with the addition of weights on the arrows
(arcs). For example the arrow from 1 to 2, would have weight 1/3.

More Examples
We now present several more examples of Markov chains arising from different application areas.
These will serve as running examples throughout the rest of the chapter.
Consider two containers, A and B, holding a total of N particles
between them. At each time step, a particle is chosen uniformly at random (from all N particles)
and moved to the other container. Let Xn denote the number of particles in container A after n steps.
Then X0 , X1 , X2 , . . . is a Markov chain with state space S = {0, 1, . . . , N}.
Example (Ehrenfest Chain).

The transition probabilities are:
• From state i, the probability of moving to state i − 1 is i/N (choosing one of the i particles
in A).
• From state i, the probability of moving to state i + 1 is (N − i)/N (choosing one of the N − i
particles in B).

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

All other transitions have probability zero. This model was introduced by Paul and Tatiana Ehrenfest in 1907 to study heat exchange between two bodies.

Formal Definitions
Let us now define a Markov chain in a slightly more precise manner.
Definition : Markov Chain — A collection of random variables X0 , X1 , X2 . . . is called a Markov

chain with state space S if
P (Xn+1 = xn+1 | X0 = x0 , . . . , Xn = xn ) = P (Xn+1 = xn+1 | Xn = xn )

(4.54)

for all x0 , . . . , xn+1 ∈ S and n ∈ N. Equation (4.54) is called the Markov property.

We restrict ourselves to Markov chains for which the conditional probability
P (Xn+1 = j | Xn = i), i, j ∈ S

(4.55)

is independent of the time n; such chains are called time-homogeneous. We also restrict ourselves
to the case where the state space S is discrete (countable), like in Example 4.4. The probabilities in
(4.55) are called the (one-step) transition probabilities of X := {Xn , n ⩾ 0}. The distribution of X0
is called the initial distribution of the Markov chain. The one-step transition probabilities and the
initial distribution completely specify the distribution of X. Namely, we have by the product rule

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

94

(4.17) and the Markov property (4.54)
P (X0 = x0 , . . . , Xn = xn )
= P (X0 = x0 ) P (X1 = x1 | X0 = x0 ) · · · P (Xn = xn | X0 = x0 , . . . Xn−1 = xn−1 )
= P (X0 = x0 ) P (X1 = x1 | X0 = x0 ) · · · P (Xn = xn | Xn−1 = xn−1 ).
The probability of simulating the “path” {X0 = 1, X1 =
2, X2 = 3, X3 = 4, X4 = 6, X5 = 5} is: 1 × 1/3 × 1/2 × 1/2 × 1 × 1/2 = 1/24.
Example (Stepping Stones (Continued)).

customers, i.e., K = ∞? The state space becomes S = {0, 1, 2, . . .} and the transition probabilities
for i ⩾ 1 are
Example (Queueing with Unlimited Buffer).

pi,i+1 = λ,

pi,i−1 = µ,

pi,i = 1 − λ − µ,

(4.56)

Intuitively, if customers arrive “faster” than they are served (λ > µ), the queue grows without

the chain has a proper limiting distribution if and only if λ < µ.

(4.59)

Each row has exactly two nonzero entries: q on the sub-diagonal and p on the super-diagonal.
Let X start at 0, thus P (X0 = 0) = 1. The corresponding transition graph is given in Figure 4.12.
Starting at 0, we take subsequent steps to the right with probability p and to the left with probability
q.

Figure 4.12: Transition graph for the random walk on Z.
Unlike the queueing model, the state space here is all of Z: there is no boundary, and the chain
can drift arbitrarily far in either direction.
The random walk on Z generalises naturally
to higher dimensions. A simple symmetric random walk on Z2 (the integer lattice in the plane)
starts at the origin (0, 0) and at each step moves to one of the four neighbouring lattice points —
up, down, left, or right — each with probability 1/4. Similarly, a simple symmetric random walk
on Z3 moves at each step to one of the six neighbouring points, each with probability 1/6.

Example (Random Walks in Higher Dimensions).

Copyright © 2026

4

Figure 4.13: The simple symmetric random walk on Z2 : from any lattice point, the walk moves to
each of the four neighbours with equal probability.
A natural question is: starting at the origin, will the random walk ever return? A celebrated
result of George Pólya (1921) gives a surprising answer that depends on the dimension.
The simple symmetric random walk starting at the origin
returns to the origin with probability 1 in dimensions d = 1 and d = 2. However, in dimension
d = 3 (and all higher dimensions), there is a positive probability of never returning. In the language
of Section 4.7, the walk is recurrent in one and two dimensions but transient in three or more
dimensions.
Remark (Pólya’s Recurrence Theorem).

Concretely, for the three-dimensional walk the probability of ever returning to the origin is
approximately 0.34: roughly two times out of three, a random walk in three-dimensional space
wanders off to infinity and never comes back. This is sometimes paraphrased as: “a drunk man will
find his way home, but a drunk bird may get lost forever.”

Simulating Markov Chains
To simulate a Markov chain with initial distribution p(0) (a row vector with non-negative entries
summing to 1) and transition matrix P, we proceed as follows:
Algorithm 4.4.1: Generating a Markov Chain
1. Draw X0 from the initial distribution p(0) . Set n = 0.
2. Draw Xn+1 from the distribution corresponding to the Xn -th row of P.
3. Set n = n + 1 and go to 2.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Simulating the stepping stones Markov chain

LLM Prompt > Write Python code that simulates 101 steps of a Markov chain with a given 6 × 6

transition matrix (the stepping stones example), starting from state 1, and plots the state trajectory.

Figure 4.14: A path of the stepping stone Markov chain.

Consider the random walk on the integers in Example 4.4. Let X0 = 0 (that is, we start at 0). Suppose the chain is at some time n in state i.
Then, in step 2 of Algorithm 4.4.1 we simply need to draw from a 2-point distribution with mass p
and q at i + 1 and i − 1 respectively. In other words, we draw In ∼ Ber(p) and set Xn+1 = Xn + 2In − 1.
Note that when simulating a Markov chain we do not always have to use the transition matrix explicitly. Whenever the transition from Xn to Xn+1 can be described by a simple algorithmic rule —
as here, where we just add +1 or −1 — we can use that rule directly. This is especially important
when the state space (and hence the matrix) is infinite.

4.5

Markov Chains and Matrix Computations

In the previous section we computed the distribution of X1 , X2 , X3 for the two-state chain step by
step, using the law of total probability. In this section we show that these calculations are really
just matrix multiplications.
Throughout this section we focus on finite state spaces: S = {1, 2, . . . , r} for some fixed r, so
that the transition matrix P is an r × r matrix.

The n-Step Transition Probability Matrix
The key observation is that P2 — the ordinary matrix square of P — is the two-step transition
matrix: its (i, j)-entry is the probability of going from state i to state j in exactly two steps. To see
why, apply the law of total probability, summing over the intermediate state ℓ:
P (X2 = j | X0 = i) =

r
X

P (X2 = j | X1 = ℓ) P (X1 = ℓ | X0 = i) =

ℓ=1

r
X

Pi,ℓ Pℓ, j .

(4.60)

ℓ=1

The right-hand side is precisely the (i, j)-entry of the matrix product P · P = P2 . (We write Pi, j for
the (i, j)-entry of a matrix P, and similarly v j for the j-th component of a vector v.) More generally,
the same argument applied one step at a time (i.e. by induction) shows that
P (Xn = j | X0 = i) = Pni, j ,

(4.61)

the (i, j)-entry of Pn . Thus Pn is the n-step transition matrix: to find the probability of going from
state i to state j in n steps, we simply read off the (i, j)-entry of Pn .

Computing the Stationary Distribution
Denote the common row of the limiting matrix P∞ by π; informally, π = p(∞) , the distribution the
chain settles into after infinitely many steps. By Claim 21, we have p(n+1) = p(n) P. If p(n) → π, then
taking the limit gives
X
π = π P,
with π j ⩾ 0 and
π j = 1.
(4.68)
j

In other words, π is a fixed point of the operation “multiply by P”: once the distribution reaches π,
one more step leaves it unchanged. We call π the stationary distribution. To find it, we solve a
system of linear equations.
 0.1 
Example (Machine Reliability (Continued)). For the machine reliability model with P = 0.9
0.4 0.6 ,
the equation π = π P gives
π1 = 0.9 π1 + 0.4 π2 ,

π2 = 0.1 π1 + 0.6 π2 .

(4.69)

From the first equation, 0.1 π1 = 0.4 π2 , so π1 = 4 π2 . Together with π1 + π2 = 1, we get π2 = 1/5
and π1 = 4/5, giving π = (0.8, 0.2). This matches exactly the rows of P50 computed above: in the
long run, the machine is working 80% of the time and broken 20% of the time.

Writing π = (π1 , π2 , π3 ), the equation π = π P gives three equations (one for each state):
π1 = 0.5 π1 + 0.1 π2 + 0.4 π3 ,
π2 = 0.3 π1 + 0.6 π2 + 0.2 π3 ,
π3 = 0.2 π1 + 0.3 π2 + 0.4 π3 .

(4.70)

(4.71)
(4.72)
(4.73)

Together with the constraint π1 + π2 + π3 = 1, this is a system of linear equations. Any one of the
three equations above is redundant (since the rows of P sum to 1), so we drop one and solve. For
instance, rearranging the first two:
0.5 π1 − 0.1 π2 − 0.4 π3 = 0,
−0.3 π1 + 0.4 π2 − 0.2 π3 = 0,
π1 + π2 + π3 = 1.

(4.74)
(4.75)
(4.76)

Solving gives π ≈ (0.316, 0.386, 0.298). This means that regardless of where the chain starts, in the
long run it spends roughly 31.6% of its time in state 1, 38.6% in state 2, and 29.8% in state 3.
These observations suggest a general pattern. In general, the matrix powers Pn may or may not
converge as n → ∞, and when they do, the limiting matrix need not have all rows equal. However,
in the special case where the Markov chain has a finite state space, is irreducible (meaning it is
possible to get from any state to any other state), and is aperiodic (the chain does not cycle with a
fixed period), then Pn converges to a matrix P∞ whose rows are all equal to some probability vector
p. This p is called the limiting distribution of the Markov chain. The fact that all rows are equal
has a concrete probabilistic meaning: limn→∞ P (Xn = i) = pi regardless of the starting state, so the
chain “forgets” where it started. To find p, we can read off any row of P∞ .
There are, however, many important subcases where this simple picture breaks down: the chain
may not be able to reach every state from every other state, or it may exhibit periodic behaviour, or
the state space may be infinite. These situations require a more careful analysis and are topics of
further study. The next section gives a brief glimpse of some of these issues; the course STAT3004
treats them in much greater depth.

4.6

A Peek at Markov Chain Properties

In the previous section we observed that the matrix powers Pn of several Markov chains converge
to a limiting matrix with equal rows. But does this always happen? If so, under what conditions?
In this section we develop the vocabulary needed to answer these questions.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

103

Communicating Classes
Definition : Accessibility — State j is accessible from state i (written i → j) if there exists some
n ⩾ 0 such that Pni, j > 0. In other words, starting from i, there is a positive probability of reaching j in
some number of steps.
Definition : Communication — States i and j communicate (written i ↔ j) if i → j and j → i; that

is, each state is accessible from the other.

Communication is an equivalence relation: it is reflexive (i ↔ i, since P0i,i = 1), symmetric (by
definition), and transitive (if i ↔ j and j ↔ k, then i ↔ k). Therefore, communication partitions
the state space into disjoint communicating classes.
Consider the stepping stones chain from Example 4.4. From the
transition matrix, we can verify that every state can be reached from every other state: for instance,
to go from state 1 to state 4 we can use the direct transition 1 → 4 (one step), and to go from state 4
to state 1 we can go 4 → 6 → 3 → 1 (three steps). Since all states communicate, there is a single
communicating class: {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}.

Example (Stepping Stones).

The Ehrenfest chain (Example 4.4) with N = 4 also has a single
communicating class: from any state i we can reach state 0 by moving particles one at a time from
A to B, and from state 0 we can reach any state by the reverse process.
Example (Ehrenfest Chain).

Definition : Irreducible Markov Chain — A Markov chain is irreducible if all states communicate
with each other, i.e., the entire state space forms a single communicating class.

The stepping stones chain, the two-state chain from Example 4.4, the Ehrenfest chain, the
machine reliability model, and the queueing model are all irreducible.
Not all Markov chains are irreducible. For instance, consider the transition matrix


1/2 1/2 0


P = 1/2 1/2 0 .


0
0 1

(4.77)

Here states 1 and 2 communicate with each other, but state 3 is not accessible from states 1 or 2,
and vice versa. The communicating classes are {1, 2} and {3}. Moreover, state 3 is an absorbing
state: once the chain enters state 3, it stays there forever.

Recurrent and Transient States
Definition : Recurrence and Transience — A state i is recurrent if, starting from i, the chain

returns to i with probability 1. A state i is transient if, starting from i, there is a positive probability of
never returning to i.

Intuitively, the chain visits a recurrent state infinitely often (since each time it returns, it will
return again with probability 1), while it visits a transient state only finitely many times.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Conditional Probability and Markov Chains

104

The following key results connect communicating classes to recurrence:
• All states in the same communicating class are either all recurrent or all transient.
• In a finite irreducible Markov chain, all states are recurrent.
The second result makes intuitive sense: in a finite state space where every state can reach every
other, the chain must keep revisiting states — it has nowhere to “escape to”.
Example (Random Walk on Z). The random walk on Z from Example 4.4 is irreducible (every
state can reach every other state). When p = q = 1/2, all states are recurrent — the symmetric
random walk always returns to the origin. However, when p , 1/2, all states are transient — the
walk drifts off to +∞ or −∞ and never returns. This shows that recurrence depends on the transition
probabilities, not just the structure of the transition graph.

Limiting Distributions
In Section 4.5 we observed that for several examples, Pn converges to a matrix with equal rows.
We now state this more precisely.
The limiting behaviour of Markov chains as n → ∞, i.e., after the process has been going on for
a very long time, is of considerable interest and importance. The results are often much simpler and
are quite useful in approximating real situations. It can be shown that for general Markov chains
(satisfying some mild conditions)
lim Pni, j = π j ,

n→∞

(4.78)

for some number 0 ⩽ π j ⩽ 1. When the {π j } sum up to 1, they form the limiting distribution of
the Markov chain. The following theorem gives a method for obtaining limiting distributions. Here
we assume for simplicity that S = {0, 1, 2, . . .}. The limiting distribution is identified with the row
vector π = (π0 , π1 , . . .).
Claim 22: Stationary Distribution — The limiting distribution π, if it exists, is uniquely determined

by the solution of
π = πP,

(4.79)

with π j ⩾ 0 and j π j = 1. Conversely, if there exists a unique positive row vector π satisfying (4.79)
and summing up to 1, then π is the limiting distribution of the Markov chain.
P

since the y j ’s sum up to unity. We omit the proof of the converse statement.

The equation π = πP says that π is a left eigenvector of P with eigenvalue 1. Equivalently, it
says that if the chain’s distribution at some time is π, then after one more step the distribution is
still π — the chain is in equilibrium.
Definition : Period and Aperiodicity — The period of a state i is di = gcd{n ⩾ 1 : Pni,i > 0}, the

greatest common divisor of all return times. A state with period 1 is called aperiodic. If all states of an
irreducible chain are aperiodic, the chain itself is called aperiodic.

For example, a chain that can only return to a state at even times has period 2. Self-loops
(Pi,i > 0) guarantee aperiodicity.
For finite irreducible aperiodic chains, the limiting distribution always exists and is unique. All
the examples we have seen in this chapter (stepping stones, two-state chain, reliability, queueing)
satisfy these conditions. (The Ehrenfest chain is irreducible but has period 2, since it alternates
between even and odd states.)
To find the limiting distribution π we need to solve the

CHAPTER

5

C ONTINUOUS R ANDOM VARIABLES AND
THEIR D ISTRIBUTIONS

In this chapter we introduce continuous distributions, their expectation, moments, and properties.
We focus on families of continuous distributions including the Normal distribution, exponential,
uniform, Gamma, Beta, Pareto, Cauchy, and a few others.

5.1

Continuous Random Variables and Their Distributions

In Chapter 3 we studied discrete random variables, whose possible values form a finite or countably
infinite set of isolated points. We now turn to continuous random variables, which can take any
value in an interval (or union of intervals). Examples include the exact time until a phone call
arrives, the height of a randomly chosen person.

Reviewing the Cumulative Distribution Function
Recall from Chapter 3 that the cumulative distribution function (cdf) of any random variable X is
F(x) = P (X ⩽ x),

x ∈ R.

Every cdf satisfies the same basic properties, whether X is discrete or continuous:
1. 0 ⩽ F(x) ⩽ 1 for all x,
2. F is non-decreasing,
3. lim F(x) = 0 and lim F(x) = 1,
x→−∞

x→+∞

4. P (a < X ⩽ b) = F(b) − F(a) for any a < b.

107

(5.1)

Continuous Random Variables and their Distributions

108

For a discrete random variable, the cdf is a step function: it is constant between the support
points and has a jump at each value xk of size P (X = xk ). The key difference for a continuous
random variable is that the cdf has no jumps — it is a continuous function.
Definition : Continuous Random Variable — A random variable X is continuous if there exists a

non-negative function f such that
F(x) =

Z x
f (t) dt,

for all x ∈ R.

(5.2)

−∞

The function f is called the probability density function (pdf) of X.

In simple terms, we can think of a continuous random variable as one whose cdf F is a continuous function (no jumps). There do exist exotic examples of random variables with a continuous cdf
that nonetheless have no density, but such cases are beyond the scope of this course.
Since the jump size of F at x equals P (X = x), a continuous cdf means:
P (X = x) = 0

for every x ∈ R.

(5.3)

In words: a continuous random variable assigns probability zero to every individual value. This
may seem paradoxical at first — if every value has probability zero, how can X take any value
at all? The resolution is that probability is spread continuously over an interval, like mass spread
smoothly over a rod. No single point carries any mass, yet the total mass over any interval of
positive length is positive.
A useful consequence is that for continuous random variables, strict and non-strict inequalities
do not matter:
P (a ⩽ X ⩽ b) = P (a < X < b) = P (a ⩽ X < b) = P (a < X ⩽ b) = F(b) − F(a).

(5.4)

This is because P (X = a) = P (X = b) = 0, so including or excluding the endpoints makes no
difference. (For discrete random variables this is not true — boundary points can carry positive
probability.)

The Probability Density Function
For a discrete random variable, we described the distribution via the probability mass function
f (x) = P (X = x). But for a continuous random variable, P (X = x) = 0 for every x, so the pmf is
identically zero — it tells us nothing. We need a different tool.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Continuous Random Variables and their Distributions

109

The idea is to look at probabilities over small intervals instead of single points. For a small
positive number h,
P (x < X ⩽ x + h) = F(x + h) − F(x).

(5.5)

If F is differentiable at x, then by the definition of the derivative,
F(x + h) − F(x) = F ′ (x) · h + o(h) ≈ F ′ (x) · h

(5.6)

for small h, where o(h) denotes terms that vanish faster than h. In other words,
P (x < X ⩽ x + h) ≈ F ′ (x) · h.

(5.7)

The derivative F ′ (x) measures how fast probability accumulates near x: it is the probability per unit
length at x.
By the fundamental theorem of calculus, if F is differentiable (except possibly at finitely many
points), the pdf can be recovered as
f (x) = F ′ (x) =

dF
(x).
dx

(5.8)

Since F is non-decreasing, its derivative is non-negative: f (x) ⩾ 0. And since F rises from 0 to
1, the total area under the density curve is
Z ∞
f (x) dx = F(∞) − F(−∞) = 1 − 0 = 1.
(5.9)
−∞

Conversely, the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus recovers the cdf from the pdf:
Z x
F(x) =
f (t) dt.

(5.10)

−∞

Probabilities are computed as areas under the density curve:
Z b
P (a < X ⩽ b) =
f (x) dx = F(b) − F(a).

(5.11)

a

In summary, the pdf and cdf are related by differentiation and integration:
Z x

f =F
and
F(x) =
f (t) dt.

(5.12)

−∞

Unlike the pmf, the value f (x) is not the probability that
X equals x (that probability is always zero). Rather, f (x) is a density: probability per unit length.
In particular, f (x) can exceed 1. What must equal 1 is the total area under f , not the height.

Remark (The density is not a probability).

Any function f : R → R satisfying
1. f (x) ⩾ 0 for all x, and

the support contributes to the integral in property 2.

5.2

Expectation, Moments, and Properties

The definitions of expectation, variance, and moments carry over from the discrete case (Section 3.2) with one systematic change: sums become integrals. All algebraic properties — linearity
of expectation, the computational formula for variance, and variance under linear transformations
— are identical, since their proofs rely only on the linearity of the expectation operator.

Properties of Expectation and Variance
The key algebraic properties established in Section 3.2 hold identically for continuous random
variables. The proofs are the same, with sums replaced by integrals.
Claim 24: Properties of Expectation and Variance (Continuous) — Let X be a continuous ran-

dom variable with pdf f , and let a, b ∈ R be constants. Then:
1. Linearity of expectation:
E[aX + b] = a E[X] + b.

(5.28)

2. Additivity of expectation: For any functions g, h : R → R,
E[g(X) + h(X)] = E[g(X)] + E[h(X)].

(5.29)

3. Variance under linear transformation:
Var(aX + b) = a2 Var(X).

(5.30)

4. Computational formula for variance:


(5.31)

Other Descriptors
Beyond the mean and variance (which are linked to the first two moments), higher moments and
quantile-based quantities provide additional information about the shape of a distribution. These
concepts apply to both discrete and continuous random variables; for continuous distributions, the
quantile definitions are particularly clean because the cdf is continuous.
Skewness. The skewness is based on the third central moment and measures asymmetry of the
distribution about its mean:


A symmetric distribution has γ1 = 0. If γ1 > 0 the distribution is right-skewed (longer right tail); if
γ1 < 0 it is left-skewed.
Kurtosis. The kurtosis is based on the fourth central moment and measures the heaviness of the
tails relative to a normal distribution:


E (X − µ)4

.

The normal distribution has κ = 3. The excess kurtosis κ − 3 is often reported instead, so that the
normal serves as the baseline with excess kurtosis 0.
Median and Quantiles. The median is the value m such that F(m) = 12 . More generally, the
q-th quantile (for 0 < q < 1) is the value xq satisfying F(xq ) = q. Important special cases include
the first quartile x0.25 , the median x0.5 , and the third quartile x0.75 .

5.3

The Normal Distribution

The normal (or Gaussian) distribution is the most important distribution in the study of statistics.
One of the main reasons for its importance is the central limit theorem (Chapter 10), which (roughly
speaking) states that the sum of a large number of independent random variables is approximately
normally distributed, regardless of the distributions of the individual terms. We will study this
remarkable result in Chapter 10; for now, we develop the properties of the normal distribution
itself.
We say that a random variable X has a normal distribution with parameters µ and σ2 if its
density function f is given by
f (x) =

1
1 x−µ 2
√ e− 2 ( σ ) ,
σ 2π

x ∈ R.

(5.47)

We write X ∼ N(µ, σ2 ). The parameters µ and σ2 turn out to be the expectation and variance of the
distribution, respectively (as we verify below).
In the notation N(µ, σ2 ), the second argument is the variance
σ , not the standard deviation σ. This is the convention used throughout these notes and in most
probability textbooks. However, many software implementations parametrise by the standard deviation instead: for example, scipy.stats.norm(loc=µ, scale=σ) and rnorm(n, mean=µ,
sd=σ) in R both take σ, not σ2 . Always check which convention is in use.

No Closed-Form CDF
Unlike the distributions we have seen so far (such as the uniform), the normal cdf
Z x
1
2
Φ(x) =
√ e−t /2 dt
−∞
2π

(5.52)

has no elementary closed form — it cannot be expressed in terms of polynomials, exponentials,
logarithms, or trigonometric functions. This is not a matter of ingenuity; it is a theorem. Liouville’s
theorem on integration in finite terms (1835) provides a systematic criterion for deciding when an
2
antiderivative can be expressed in elementary functions, and e−t /2 provably fails this criterion.
Therefore Φ(x) must be evaluated numerically, either by consulting a table of precomputed values
(see Appendix B) or by using software (e.g., scipy.stats.norm.cdf in Python or pnorm in R).
This is the main reason why normal probability tables have historically been so important in
statistics.

Further Properties
The 68–95–99.7 rule. For any normal distribution, the probability of falling within 1, 2, or 3
standard deviations of the mean is approximately:
P (µ − σ < X < µ + σ) ≈ 0.683,

P (µ − 2σ < X < µ + 2σ) ≈ 0.954,

P (µ − 3σ < X < µ + 3σ) ≈ 0.997.

(5.60)
(5.61)

These follow from standardisation: P (µ − kσ < X < µ + kσ) = Φ(k) − Φ(−k), and the values
Φ(k) − Φ(−k) for k = 1, 2, 3 were computed in the code output above. In practice, this means that
almost all the probability mass of a normal distribution lies within three standard deviations of the
mean.
Symmetry. The pdf of N(µ, σ2 ) is symmetric about µ. As a consequence, the median and the
mode (the value where the pdf is maximised) both equal µ, coinciding with the mean. For the
standard normal, symmetry gives Φ(−z) = 1 − Φ(z) for all z, which is useful for computing tail
probabilities.
Linear transformations.

If X ∼ N(µ, σ2 ) and Y = aX + b for constants a , 0 and b, then
Y ∼ N(aµ + b, a2 σ2 ).

(5.62)

That is, linear transformations of normal random variables are again normal. This follows from
the standardisation result: Z = (X − µ)/σ ∼ N(0, 1), so Y = a(µ + σZ) + b = (aµ + b) + (aσ)Z,
which is N(aµ + b, a2 σ2 ).
Computing probabilities. Combining standardisation with the Φ table (or software), we can
compute any probability involving a normal random variable. For X ∼ N(µ, σ2 ),

The Exponential Distribution

A random variable X with probability density function f given by
f (x) = λ e−λx ,

x ⩾ 0,

(5.66)

is said to have an exponential distribution with parameter λ > 0. We write X ∼ Exp(λ). The
exponential distribution can be viewed as a continuous analogue of the geometric distribution: the
geometric counts the number of discrete trials until the first success, while the exponential models
the continuous waiting time until the first occurrence of an event. Graphs of the pdf for various
values of λ are given in Figure 5.4.

P (X > x) = e−λx ,

The function F̄(x) = 1 − F(x) = P (X > x) is called the survival function (or complementary cdf,
abbreviated CCDF). It gives the probability that X exceeds the value x — in reliability and lifetime
modelling, this is the probability of “surviving” past time x.
The survival function provides an elegant alternative formula for the expectation of a nonnegative random variable.

The memoryless property

The most important property of the exponential distribution is the following.
Claim 27: Memoryless Property — Let X ∼ Exp(λ). Then

P (X > x + y | X > x) = P (X > y),

for all x, y ⩾ 0.

(5.76)

Proof: By the definition of conditional probability,
P (X > x + y, X > x) P (X > x + y)
=
(5.77)
P (X > x)
P (X > x)
e−λ(x+y)
= −λx = e−λy = P (X > y),
(5.78)
e
where we used that the event {X > x + y} is contained in {X > x}, so their intersection is {X > x + y}.
□
P (X > x + y | X > x) =

For example, if X denotes the lifetime of a machine, then given that the machine is still alive at
time x, the remaining lifetime X − x has the same exponential distribution as a completely new machine. In other words, the machine has no memory of its age and does not “deteriorate” (although
it will break down eventually).
This is exactly the continuous-time analogue of the memoryless property of the geometric distribution. Recall from Section 3.4 that T ∼ Geom(p) satisfies P (T > m + n | T > m) = P (T > n):
given that the first success has not occurred in the first m trials, the remaining waiting time has
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Continuous Random Variables and their Distributions

125

the same distribution as starting fresh. The exponential plays the same role for continuous waiting
times that the geometric plays for discrete ones.
It is not difficult to prove that the exponential distribution is the only continuous distribution on
(0, ∞) with the memoryless property, just as the geometric is the only discrete distribution with this
property.

5.5

The Gamma and Beta Distributions

Before introducing the gamma and beta families of distributions, we discuss the special functions
that give them their names. These functions arise throughout mathematics — in combinatorics,
number theory, complex analysis, and physics — and are not specific to probability theory. However, they play a central role in normalising the density functions we study here.

The Gamma and Beta Functions
The Gamma function. The gamma function is defined by
Z ∞

uα−1 e−u du, α > 0.

0

This integral converges for all α > 0 and defines a smooth, strictly positive function on (0, ∞). The
gamma function extends the factorial to non-integer arguments: as we now show, Γ(n) = (n − 1)!
for every positive integer n.

Reparametrisation by Mean and Coefficient of Variation
The gamma distribution is often parametrised by the shape α and rate λ, but in applications — particularly in manufacturing and reliability engineering — it is more natural to describe a distribution
by its mean and its relative spread. The coefficient of variation (CV) of a positive random variable
X is defined as the ratio of the standard deviation to the mean:
SD(X) σ

Special Cases of the Gamma Distribution
The gamma family is remarkably versatile: several important named distributions are special cases.
Exponential distribution. Taking α = 1 in the gamma pdf (5.87) gives
f (x) = λ e−λx ,

x ⩾ 0,

(5.94)

which is precisely the Exp(λ) density from Section 5.4. Thus
Exp(λ) = Gamma(1, λ).

(5.95)

This is consistent with the mean and variance: E[X] = 1/λ and Var(X) = 1/λ2 .

Erlang distribution. When the shape parameter α is a positive integer, say α = k with k ∈

Erl(k, λ) = Gamma(k, λ),

k ∈ {1, 2, 3, . . .}.

x ⩾ 0.

(5.99)

The Erlang distribution has a natural interpretation: if T 1 , T 2 , . . . , T k are independent Exp(λ) random variables (e.g., inter-arrival times in a Poisson process), then their sum S k = T 1 + · · · + T k has
an Erl(k, λ) distribution. This is the waiting time until the k-th event — compare with the negative
binomial distribution, which counts the number of discrete trials until the k-th success.
In summary, the relationships are:
Exp(λ) = Gamma(1, λ) = Erl(1, λ),
Erl(k, λ) = Gamma(k, λ)

The Beta Distribution
A random variable X has a beta distribution with parameters α > 0 and β > 0 if its density
function is
f (x) =

xα−1 (1 − x)β−1
,
B(α, β)

0 ⩽ x ⩽ 1,

(5.101)

where B(α, β) = Γ(α)Γ(β)/Γ(α + β) is the beta function defined in (5.84). We write X ∼ Beta(α, β).
The beta distribution is supported on [0, 1], making it a natural model for proportions, probabilities,
and other quantities constrained to lie between 0 and 1.
The shape of the beta density depends strongly on the parameters α and β, as illustrated in
Figure 5.8. When both parameters are less than 1, the density is U-shaped; when both are greater

Transformations of the Beta Distribution
The standard beta distribution is supported on [0, 1], but in applications we often need a random
variable supported on a general interval [a, b]. This is achieved by a simple linear transformation.

CHAPTER

6

M ORE A SPECTS OF R ANDOM VARIABLES
AND D ISTRIBUTIONS

In this chapter we explore several additional aspects of random variables and their distributions.
We develop techniques for finding the distribution of a transformed random variable, and see how
relationships between families of probability distributions arise. We then consider the inverse probability transform, a universal method for simulating random variables. We establish the Markov
and Chebyshev inequalities, which bound tail probabilities using only moments. Finally, we encounter random variables whose moments are infinite or even undefined — the Pareto and Cauchy
distributions.

6.1

Transformations of Random Variables

Given a random variable X with a known distribution, we often need to determine the distribution of
a transformed random variable Y = g(X) for some function g. This is one of the most fundamental
operations in probability and statistics.

What We Already Know
Before developing the general theory, let us recall what tools we already have at our disposal.
Computing expectations without finding the distribution. Thanks to LOTUS (Claims 8
and 23), we can compute E[g(X)] directly from the distribution of X, without ever finding the

132

More Aspects of Random Variables and Distributions

distribution of Y = g(X):
X
E[g(X)] =
g(x) fX (x)
x

(discrete),

E[g(X)] =

133

Z ∞
g(x) fX (x) dx

(continuous).

(6.1)

−∞

This is extremely useful when all we need is the mean (or higher moments) of Y. However, there
are many situations where we need the full distribution of Y — its pdf, cdf, or variance — and for
that we need the techniques developed in this section.
Linear transformations. We have already handled the special case Y = aX + b (with a , 0) for
several distribution families:
• Normal: If X ∼ N(µ, σ2 ), then aX + b ∼ N(aµ + b, a2 σ2 ). In particular, standardisation
Z = (X − µ)/σ ∼ N(0, 1) is a linear transformation (Claim 25).
• Uniform: If U ∼ U(0, 1), then X = a + (b − a)U ∼ U(a, b). The uniform on any interval is
obtained by a linear transformation of the standard uniform (Section 5.2).
• Geometric: The two conventions for the geometric distribution — counting trials (X ∼
Geom(p) with support {1, 2, . . .}) and counting failures (Y = X − 1 with support {0, 1, . . .}) —
are related by a simple shift Y = X − 1 (Section 3.4).
• General: For any random variable, E[aX + b] = a E[X] + b and Var(aX + b) = a2 Var(X)
(see (5.30)).
But what about other
distributions and, more generally, nonlinear transformations such as Y =
√
X
X , Y = e , or Y = X? For these we need a systematic method.
2

The CDF Method
The most general and conceptually simple approach is to work through the cdf. Since we now deal
with multiple random variables simultaneously, we use subscripts to distinguish their distributions:
F X and fX denote the cdf and pdf of X, while FY and fY denote those of Y. (When there is only one
random variable in play, we continue to write simply F and f .) If Y = g(X), then


FY (y) = P (Y ⩽ y) = P g(X) ⩽ y = P {ω : g(X(ω)) ⩽ y} .
(6.2)
We rewrite the event {g(X) ⩽ y} in terms of X and compute the probability using the known distribution of X. This approach always works, regardless of whether g is monotone, one-to-one,
or anything else. In the continuous case we can go further and differentiate to obtain the pdf
fY (y) = FY′ (y).

One-to-One Transformations: The Change-of-Variable Formula
When g is a strictly monotone (and hence one-to-one) differentiable function, there is a convenient
shortcut.

Proof: Suppose first that g is strictly increasing. Then g has an inverse g−1 , and since g(X(ω)) ⩽ y
if and only if X(ω) ⩽ g−1 (y),
FY (y) = P (g(X) ⩽ y) = P (X ⩽ g−1 (y)) = F X (g−1 (y)).

fY (y) = FY′ (y) = fX (g−1 (y)) ·

(6.5)

Since g is increasing, g−1 is also increasing, so dyd g−1 (y) > 0 and the absolute value is not needed.
If instead g is strictly decreasing, then g(X(ω)) ⩽ y if and only if X(ω) ⩾ g−1 (y), so
FY (y) = P (g(X) ⩽ y) = P (X ⩾ g−1 (y)) = 1 − F X (g−1 (y)),

(6.6)

so fY (y) = − fX (g−1 (y)) · dyd g−1 (y). Since g−1 is decreasing, the derivative is negative, and the minus
sign makes the result positive. In both cases, the formula fY (y) = fX (g−1 (y)) · dyd g−1 (y) holds. □
Intuition. The factor |(d/dy) g−1 (y)| accounts for the “stretching” or “compression” that g applies:
regions where g stretches the x-axis get lower density (probability is spread over a wider range of
y), while regions where g compresses get higher density.

6.2

The Inverse Probability Transform

In Chapter 3 we developed the cumulative probability method (Algorithm 3.6.1) for simulating
discrete random variables: partition [0, 1) into sub-intervals of lengths p1 , p2 , . . ., draw U ∼ U(0, 1),
and return whichever value corresponds to the sub-interval that U falls in. In this section we
show that this idea generalises to a beautiful and universal principle — the inverse probability
transform — which works for any distribution, discrete or continuous.

Continuous Distributions Supported on R
We begin with the cleanest case. Suppose X is a continuous random variable whose pdf is positive
for every x ∈ R (for example, the normal distribution). Then the cdf F is strictly increasing on all
of R, rising continuously from 0 to 1. This means F is a bijection from R onto (0, 1), and it has a
well-defined inverse
F −1 : (0, 1) → R.

(6.24)

The key observation is: if U ∼ U(0, 1), then the random variable X = F −1 (U) has cdf F. We prove
this below in the slightly more general setting that also covers distributions with restricted support.

Continuous Distributions Supported on a Subset of R
Many continuous distributions have support on a proper subset of R, such as [0, ∞) (exponential,
gamma, Rayleigh) or a bounded interval [a, b] (uniform). In these cases the cdf F is constant
outside the support — equal to 0 below the support and 1 above it — but strictly increasing on
the support. The cdf still maps the support one-to-one onto (0, 1), and the inverse F −1 : (0, 1) →
support is well-defined. The same principle applies.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

More Aspects of Random Variables and Distributions

138

Claim 29: Inverse probability transform (continuous case) — Let F be a cdf that is continuous

and strictly increasing on the support of the distribution, and let U ∼ U(0, 1). Then
X = F −1 (U)

has cdf F.

(6.25)

Proof: Since F is strictly increasing and continuous on the support, F −1 is well-defined, and
F −1 (u) ⩽ x if and only if u ⩽ F(x). Therefore,
P (X ⩽ x) = P (F −1 (U) ⩽ x) = P (U ⩽ F(x)) = F(x),

(6.26)

where the last equality uses the cdf of the U(0, 1) distribution:

(6.27)

Since F(x) ∈ [0, 1], we have P (U ⩽ F(x)) = F(x).

□

Claim 29 immediately gives us a simulation recipe: to generate a random variable with any
desired continuous cdf F, simply draw U ∼ U(0, 1) and return X = F −1 (U). The method is
illustrated graphically in Figure 6.2: given a value u on the vertical axis, we read off x = F −1 (u) on
the horizontal axis.

The Quantile Function and the General Case
The inverse cdf F −1 is also known as the quantile function. Recall from Section 5.2 that the p-th
quantile of a distribution is the value x p satisfying F(x p ) = p. In the notation of the inverse cdf,
this is simply x p = F −1 (p). For example, F −1 (0.5) is the median, F −1 (0.25) is the first quartile,
and F −1 (0.75) is the third quartile. The inverse probability transform can therefore be read as: a
random quantile, evaluated at a uniform random probability level, gives a random variable with
the desired distribution.
Not every cdf is continuous and strictly increasing. A discrete cdf has jumps, and even some
continuous cdfs have flat regions. To handle all cases, we define the generalised inverse of any cdf
F as
F −1 (u) = inf{x : F(x) ⩾ u},

0 < u < 1.

(6.39)

This is the proper definition of the quantile function: it is well-defined for any cdf, and for a
continuous, strictly increasing F it coincides with the ordinary inverse.
Claim 30: Inverse probability transform (general case) — Let F be any cdf and let U ∼ U(0, 1).

Then X = F −1 (U), with F −1 as defined in (6.39), has cdf F.

The proof in the general case uses the key property that F −1 (u) ⩽ x if and only if u ⩽ F(x)
(which holds for any cdf), and then proceeds exactly as before.

x ⩾ 2.
Example (Generalised inverse of a step-function cdf).

Since F has jumps, there is no ordinary inverse. Applying the generalised inverse F −1 (u) = inf{x :
F(x) ⩾ u}, we ask: for each value of u ∈ (0, 1], what is the smallest x at which F(x) first reaches or
exceeds u?
• If 0 < u ⩽ p0 : the cdf first reaches u at x = 0 (where F jumps to p0 ), so F −1 (u) = 0.
• If p0 < u ⩽ p0 + p1 : F(x) ⩾ u is not satisfied until x = 1 (where F jumps to p0 + p1 ), so
F −1 (u) = 1.
• If p0 + p1 < u ⩽ 1: F(x) ⩾ u is not satisfied until x = 2 (where F jumps to 1), so F −1 (u) = 2.
In summary:


(6.41)

Drawing U ∼ U(0, 1) and returning X = F −1 (U) therefore assigns X = k with probability equal to
the length of the corresponding sub-interval, which is exactly pk . This is the cumulative probability
method from Chapter 3 (Algorithm 3.6.1), now derived from the general theory.
The pattern in the example above extends to any discrete distribution. For a random variable
taking values x1 < x2 < x3 < · · · with probabilities p1 , p2 , p3 , . . ., the generalised inverse F −1 (u)
returns xk whenever u falls in the interval (F(xk−1 ), F(xk )], i.e., when
p1 + · · · + pk−1 < u ⩽ p1 + · · · + pk .

(6.42)

The “partition [0, 1) into sub-intervals” idea that we developed intuitively for discrete distributions
is, in fact, a special case of the inverse probability transform. The general theory unifies the discrete
and continuous cases under a single principle. Figure 6.3 illustrates the construction: the step
function cdf maps the uniform draw U to the value xk at which the cdf first reaches or exceeds U.

Limitations: When the Inverse CDF Has No Closed Form
The inverse probability transform is a universal theoretical result: it works for every distribution.
However, as a practical simulation method, it requires computing F −1 (u) — and for some important
distributions, no closed-form expression for F −1 exists.
The most prominent example is the normal distribution. The cdf of N(0, 1) is
Z x
1
2
Φ(x) =
√ e−t /2 dt,
−∞
2π

(6.43)

and this integral cannot be expressed in terms of elementary functions. Consequently, the inverse
Φ−1 (u) — also called the probit function — has no closed-form expression either. One can still
compute Φ−1 (u) numerically (and software like Python’s scipy.stats.norm.ppf does so efficiently), but this is slower than methods that avoid the inverse altogether. In practice, normal
random variables are usually generated by other techniques such as the Box–Muller transform,
which cleverly uses two uniform random variables and trigonometric functions to produce two
independent standard normals.
Similarly, the gamma and beta distributions do not have closed-form inverse cdfs (except in
special cases like the exponential). For these, specialised algorithms based on acceptance–rejection
methods (a Monte Carlo technique not covered in these notes) are typically used. It is important
to appreciate that while the inverse probability transform provides the conceptual foundation for
simulation, it is not always the most practical computational tool.
On the other hand, many distributions do have tractable
inverse cdfs, including: the exponential
√
−1
(F (u) = − ln(1 − u)/λ), the Rayleigh (F (u) = σ −2 ln(1 − u)), the Cauchy (F −1 (u) = tan(π(u −
1/2))), and the Weibull. For these, the inverse probability transform provides the simplest and most
efficient simulation method.

The Markov and Chebyshev Inequalities

In many situations we do not know the full distribution of a random variable, but we do know its
mean or variance. Remarkably, even this limited information allows us to bound tail probabilities.
The two key tools are the Markov inequality and its consequence, the Chebyshev inequality.

The Markov Inequality
The Markov inequality gives an upper bound on the probability that a nonnegative random variable
exceeds a given threshold, using only its mean.
Claim 31: Markov’s inequality — Let X be a nonnegative random variable with finite mean. Then

for any a > 0,
P (X ⩾ a) ⩽

E[X]

(6.44)

We give two proofs. The first is a direct calculation for continuous random variables; the second
uses indicator functions and works in complete generality.

The Chebyshev Inequality
By applying the Markov inequality to the nonnegative random variable (X − µ)2 , we obtain a much
more useful bound that involves both the mean and the variance.

For k = 1, the Chebyshev bound is vacuous (1 − 1/1 = 0), telling us nothing. But from k = 2
onward it becomes informative: for any distribution, at least 75% of the probability lies within 2
standard deviations of the mean, at least 88.9% within 3, and at least 93.75% within 4.
The normal distribution does much better than these bounds, which is to be expected: its tails
2
decay extremely fast (as e−x /2 ). The Chebyshev inequality must account for the worst case — distributions with much heavier tails — and so its bounds are necessarily conservative. Nevertheless,
the guarantee that no distribution can place more than 1/k2 of its mass beyond k standard deviations
from the mean is a powerful and universal statement.
Suppose exam scores have mean µ = 72 and standard deviation σ = 8,
but the shape of the distribution is unknown. What fraction of students scored between 56 and 88?
Example (Exam scores).

The interval [56, 88] is µ ± 2σ = 72 ± 16. By Chebyshev with k = 2:

4
At least 75% of students scored in this range, regardless of the distribution.
P (56 ⩽ X ⩽ 88) = P (|X − 72| ⩽ 16) ⩾ 1 −

(6.53)

Further Inequalities
The Markov and Chebyshev inequalities are the simplest members of a large family of probability
inequalities. More advanced courses cover many others, including:
• Chernoff bounds, which use the moment generating function to obtain exponentially tight
tail bounds;
• Jensen’s inequality, which relates E[g(X)] to g(E[X]) for convex functions g;
• Hoeffding’s inequality, which gives sharp concentration bounds for sums of bounded independent random variables;
• Cauchy–Schwarz and Hölder inequalities, fundamental tools in analysis and probability
alike.
These inequalities are central to fields such as information theory, machine learning, and mathematical finance. The Markov and Chebyshev inequalities are the starting point for all of them.

6.4

Heavy Tails, Infinite Moments, and Undefined Means

All the continuous distributions we met in Chapter 5 — the normal, uniform, exponential, gamma,
and beta — possess finite means and finite variances. In this section we encounter two important
families for which this is not always the case: the Pareto and Cauchy distributions, both examples
of so-called heavy-tailed distributions.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

More Aspects of Random Variables and Distributions

The Pareto Distribution
The Pareto distribution is named after the Italian economist Vilfredo Pareto, who used it to model
the distribution of wealth: a small number of individuals hold a disproportionately large share of
the total wealth. It has since found applications in many other areas — city sizes, file sizes on the
internet, earthquake magnitudes, and insurance claims — wherever extreme values are much more
likely than a normal distribution would predict.
A random variable X has a Pareto distribution with shape parameter α > 0 if its density
function is

The most striking feature of the Pareto distribution is that its moments are not always finite.
Whether E[X k ] exists depends on the relationship between k and α.

Remark on heavy tails. The study of heavy-tailed distributions is a rich topic in its own right,
with connections to extreme value theory, risk management, and network science. Besides the
Pareto, other notable heavy-tailed distributions include the log-normal, the stable distributions, and
the Cauchy distribution which we discuss next. What these distributions share is that rare, extreme
events are far more probable than they would be under a normal (or exponential) distribution.

Higher Moments Imply Lower Moments
The Pareto distribution illustrates a striking “staircase” pattern: if α = 3, the first and second
moments are finite but the third is not; if α = 3/2, the first moment is finite but the second is not.
This is not a peculiarity of the Pareto — it is a general fact about all distributions.
Claim 33: Moment hierarchy — Let X be a continuous random variable. If E[|X| s ] < ∞ for some

s > 0, then

E[|X|r ] < ∞

for all 0 < r < s.

(6.63)

In other words, the set of orders for which the absolute moment is finite is always a “downward closed”
interval: if a higher moment exists, all lower moments exist too.

Central moments. The same hierarchy holds for central moments. If E[|X − c| s ] < ∞ for some
constant c and some s > 0, then E[|X − c|r ] < ∞ for all 0 < r < s. This follows immediately by
applying the claim to the random variable Y = X − c.
Copyright © 2026

In particular, if Var(X) = E[(X − µ)2 ] < ∞ (where µ = E[X]), then E[|X − µ|r ] < ∞ for
all 0 < r < 2. And if the fourth central moment E[(X − µ)4 ] is finite (as it is for the normal
distribution), then all central moments of order less than 4 are finite.
Remark. The converse is false: finiteness of E[|X|r ] does not imply finiteness of E[|X| s ] for s > r.
The Pareto distribution provides a clean example: X ∼ Pareto(3/2) has E[X] < ∞ but E[X 2 ] = ∞.

The Cauchy Distribution
The Cauchy distribution is the most extreme example of a heavy-tailed distribution we will encounter: not only is its variance infinite, but its mean does not even exist in any meaningful sense.
A random variable X has a standard Cauchy distribution if its density function is
f (x) =

1
,
π(1 + x2 )

x ∈ R.

(6.67)

We write X ∼ Cauchy(0, 1). More generally, X ∼ Cauchy(µ, γ) has pdf f (x) = 1/[πγ(1 + ((x −
µ)/γ)2 )] with location parameter µ and scale parameter γ > 0.
The standard Cauchy density looks superficially similar to the standard normal density — both
are symmetric, bell-shaped curves centred at the origin. However, the Cauchy has much heavier
tails, as shown in Figure 6.5. At x = 5, the Cauchy density is about 1/(π · 26) ≈ 0.012, while the
normal density is approximately 1.5 × 10−6 — four orders of magnitude smaller.

Infinite mean vs. undefined mean. It is important to distinguish between two types of pathology:
• InfiniteR mean: E[X] = +∞ (or −∞). This occurs for the Pareto distribution with α ⩽ 1,
∞

one can show that lima→∞ −a x f (x) dx = 0 (by symmetry), but lima→∞ −2a x f (x) dx , 0. The
“mean” changes depending on how we take the limit, so it is genuinely undefined.

Failure of the Law of Large Numbers
For “well-behaved” distributions (those with a finite mean), the law of large numbers guarantees
that the sample mean X̄n = (X1 + · · · + Xn )/n converges to E[X] as n → ∞. We will study this
fundamental result in detail later in the course. The Cauchy distribution provides a striking example
of what can go wrong when the mean does not exist.
Figure 6.6 shows the running sample mean X̄n for n = 1, 2, . . . , 10,000 independent standard
Cauchy random variables. Unlike the normal or exponential case — where the running mean
quickly stabilises near the true mean — the Cauchy sample mean continues to jump erratically,
even after thousands of observations. Occasional extreme values (which the heavy tails make far
more likely than one might expect) cause sudden jumps in the average, and these jumps never die
out no matter how many samples are collected.

running sample mean, and observe that it does not converge (unlike distributions with a finite mean).

mean.
In fact, one can prove that for independent X1 , X2 , . . . ∼ Cauchy(0, 1), the sample mean X̄n =
(X1 + · · · + Xn )/n is itself Cauchy(0, 1) for every n. The average of n Cauchy random variables

uncertainty — the law of large numbers simply does not apply.

CHAPTER

7

J OINT D ISTRIBUTIONS

In previous chapters we studied individual random variables — their distributions, moments, and
transformations. In this chapter we consider situations where two or more random variables are
observed simultaneously. We develop the theory of joint distributions, marginal and conditional
distributions, covariance and correlation, and the multivariate normal and multinomial distributions.
We then introduce conditional expectation and the tower property (law of total expectation), and
apply these tools to random sums and the law of total variance.

7.1

Joint Distributions of Multiple Random Variables

Often a random experiment is described via more than one random variable. Examples are:
1. We select a random sample of n = 10 people and observe their lengths. Let X1 , . . . , Xn be the
individual lengths.
2. We flip a coin repeatedly. Let Xi = 1 if the ith flip is “heads” and 0 else. The experiment is
described by the sequence X1 , X2 , . . . of coin flips.
3. We randomly select a person from a large population and measure his/her weight X and
height Y.
How can we specify the behaviour of the random variables above? We should not just specify
the pdf or pmf of the individual random variables, but also say something about the “interaction”
(or lack thereof) between the random variables. For example, in the third experiment above if the
height Y is large, we expect that X is large as well. On the other hand, for the first and second
experiment it is reasonable to assume that information about one of the random variables does not
give extra information about the others. What we need to specify is the joint distribution of the
random variables.

153

Joint Distributions

154

The theory for multiple random variables is quite similar to that of a single random variable.
The most important extra feature is perhaps the concept of independence of random variables.
Independent random variables play a crucial role in stochastic modelling.

Random Vectors and the Joint CDF
Let X1 , . . . , Xn be random variables describing some random experiment. We can accumulate the
Xi ’s into a vector X = (X1 , . . . , Xn ), which we always interpret as a column vector. X is called a
random vector.
Recall from Chapters 3 and 5 that the distribution of a single random variable X is completely
specified by its cumulative distribution function. Analogously, the joint distribution of X1 , . . . , Xn
is specified by the joint cumulative distribution function F, defined by
F(x1 , . . . , xn ) = P ({X1 ⩽ x1 } ∩ · · · ∩ {Xn ⩽ xn }) = P (X1 ⩽ x1 , . . . , Xn ⩽ xn ).

(7.1)

If we know F then we can in principle derive any probability involving the Xi ’s. Note the abbreviation on the right-hand side. We will henceforth use this kind of abbreviation throughout the
notes.
Similar to the 1-dimensional case we distinguish between the case where the Xi are discrete
and continuous. The corresponding joint distributions are again called discrete and continuous,
respectively.

7.2

Joint Density and Probability Mass Functions

The joint distribution of a random vector is described by its joint pmf (in the discrete case) or joint
pdf (in the continuous case). We treat the two cases in turn, beginning with the discrete setting.

Discrete Joint Distributions
To see how things work in the discrete case, let’s start with an example.
In a box are three dice. Die 1 is a normal die; die 2 has no 6
face, but instead two 5 faces; die 3 has no 5 face, but instead two 6 faces. The experiment consists
of selecting a die at random, followed by a toss with that die. Let X be the die number that is
selected, and let Y be the face value of that die. The probabilities P (X = x, Y = y) are specified
below.

The function f : (x, y) 7→ P (X = x, Y = y) is called the joint pmf of X and Y. The following
definition is just a generalization of this.
Definition : Joint Probability Mass Function — Let X1 , . . . , Xn be discrete random variables. The

function f defined by f (x1 , . . . , xn ) = P (X1 = x1 , . . . , Xn = xn ) is called the joint probability mass
function (pmf) of X1 , . . . , Xn .

We sometimes write fX1 ,...,Xn instead of f to show that this is the pmf of the random variables
X1 , . . . , Xn . Or, if X is the corresponding random vector, we could write f X instead.
Note that, by the sum rule, if we are given the joint pmf of X1 , . . . , Xn we can in principle calculate all possible probabilities involving these random variables. For example, in the 2-dimensional
case
X
P ((X, Y) ∈ B) =
P (X = x, Y = y) ,
(7.2)
(x,y)∈B

for any subset B of possible values for (X, Y). In particular, we can find the pmf of X by summing
the joint pmf over all possible values of y:
X
P (X = x) =
P (X = x, Y = y) .
(7.3)
y

The converse is not true: from the individual distributions (so-called marginal distributions) of
X and Y we cannot in general reconstruct the joint distribution of X and Y. We are missing the
“dependency” information. E.g., in Example 7.2 we cannot reconstruct the inside of the twodimensional table if only given the column and row totals.
However, there is one important exception to this, namely when we are dealing with independent random variables. We have so far only defined what independence is for events. The following
definition says that random variables X1 , . . . , Xn are independent if the events {X1 ∈ A1 }, . . . , {Xn ∈
An } are independent for any subsets A1 , . . . , An of R. Intuitively, this means that any information
about one of them does not affect our knowledge about the others.
Definition : Independent Random Variables — The random variables X1 , . . . , Xn are called inde-

pendent if for all A1 , . . . , An , with Ai ⊆ R, i = 1, . . . , n

P (X1 ∈ A1 , . . . , Xn ∈ An ) = P (X1 ∈ A1 ) · · · P (Xn ∈ An ) .
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

(7.4)

The following claim is a direct consequence of the definition above.
Claim 34: Independent Discrete Random Variables — Discrete random variables X1 , . . . , Xn , are

independent if and only if
P (X1 = x1 , . . . , Xn = xn ) = P (X1 = x1 ) · · · P (Xn = xn ),

(7.5)

for all x1 , x2 , . . . , xn .

Proof: The necessary condition is obvious: if X1 , . . . , Xn are independent random variables, then
{X1 = x1 }, . . . , {Xn = xn } are (mutually) independent events. To prove the sufficient condition, write

(7.6)

xn ∈An

Then, if (7.5) holds, the multiple sum can be written as

x1 ∈A1

which implies that X1 , . . . , Xn are independent random variables.

now independent, each entry in the pmf table is 13 × 61 . Clearly in the first experiment not all events
{X = x} and {Y = y} are independent (why not?).

are very similar to the discrete case discussed above. Compare this with the one-dimensional case
from Chapter 5.
