Random samples. The type of model used in the previous example, i.e., X1 , . . . , Xn are independent and all have the same distribution, is the most widely used model in statistics. We say that
X1 , . . . , Xn is a random sample of size n, from some given distribution. In Example 7.2 X1 , . . . , Xn
is a random sample from a U[0, 1]-distribution. In Example 7.2 we also had a random sample, this
time from a Ber(p)-distribution. The common distribution of a random sample is sometimes called
the sampling distribution.

7.3

Marginal and Conditional Distributions

Given the joint distribution of (X, Y), we often want to know how Y behaves when we have information about X. This leads to the notion of a conditional distribution, which extends the idea of
conditional probability from Chapter 4 to the setting of random variables.

Mixed Distributions: One Continuous, One Discrete
Not every pair (X, Y) falls neatly into the all-discrete or all-continuous setting. It is perfectly possible for X to be continuous and Y to be discrete. Such a mixed pair has no single joint pdf or joint
pmf, but its distribution is fully specified by a marginal distribution for one variable together with
a conditional distribution for the other.

Expectation, Covariance, and Correlation

With the machinery of joint and conditional distributions in place, we now study expectations of
functions of several random variables, and introduce two quantities — covariance and correlation
— that measure the strength of the linear relationship between them.

Expectation of Functions of Several Random Variables

any real-valued function of X1 , . . . , Xn is the weighted average of all values that this function can
take. Specifically, if Z = g(X1 , . . . , Xn ) then in the discrete case

Claim 36: Linearity of Expectation — Suppose X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn are discrete or continuous random

variables with means µ1 , µ2 , . . . , µn . Let

Y = a + b1 X1 + b2 X2 + · · · + bn Xn

We can now prove that the expectation of a
Bin(n, p) random variable is np, without having to resort to difficult arguments. Namely, if X ∼

Example (Expectation of Bin(n, p) and Hyp(n, r, N) ).

Bin(n, p), then X can be written as the sum X1 + · · · + Xn of iid Ber(p) random variables, see

Example 7.2. Thus,

because the expectation of each Xi is p.
Notice that we do not use anywhere the independence of the {Xi }. Now, let X ∼ Hyp(n, r, N),
and let p = r/N. We can think of X as the total number of red balls when n balls are drawn from an
urn with r red balls and N − r other balls. Without loss of generality we may assume the balls are
drawn one-by-one. Let Xi = 1 if the i-th ball is red, and 0 otherwise. Then, again X = X1 + · · · + Xn ,
and each Xi ∼ Ber(p), but now the {Xi } are dependent. However, this does not affect the result
(7.47), so that the expectation of X is np = nr/N.

Expectation of Products of Independent Random Variables
Claim 37: Products of Independent Variables — If X1 , . . . , Xn are independent, then

EX1 X2 · · · Xn = EX1 EX2 · · · EXn .

Covariance
Definition : Covariance — The covariance of two random variables X and Y is defined as the number

Cov(X, Y) := E [(X − EX)(Y − EY)] .

(7.54)

The covariance is a measure for the amount of linear dependency between the variables. If
small values of X (smaller than the expected value of X) go together with small values of Y, and at
the same time large values of X go together with large values of Y, then Cov(X, Y) will be positive.
If on the other hand small values of X go together with large values of Y, and large values of X go
together with small values of Y, then Cov(X, Y) will be negative.
For easy reference we list some important properties of the variance and covariance in Table 7.1.
The proofs follow directly from the definitions of covariance and variance and the properties of the
expectation.
Table 7.1: Properties of variance and covariance

1.

Var(X) = EX 2 − (EX)2 .

2.

Var(aX + b) = a2 Var(X).

3.

Cov(X, Y) = EXY − EXEY.

4.

Cov(X, Y) = Cov(Y, X).

5.

Cov(aX + bY, Z) = a Cov(X, Z) + b Cov(Y, Z).

6.

Cov(X, X) = Var(X).

7.

Var(X + Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y) + 2 Cov(X, Y).

8.

X and Y indep. =⇒ Cov(X, Y) = 0.

Proof: Properties 1. and 2. were already proved in Chapters 3 and 5 (see (5.30)). Properties 4. and
6. follow directly from the definitions of covariance and variance. Denote for convenience µX = EX
and µY = EY.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Joint Distributions

167

3. Cov(X, Y) = E[(X −µX )(Y −µY )] = E[XY −YµX − XµY +µX µY ] = EXY −µY µX −µX µY +µX µY =
EXY − µX µY .
5. Using property 3. we get
Cov(aX + bY, Z) = E[(aX + bY)Z] − E[aX + bY] E[Z]
= a E[XZ] + b E[YZ] − a E[X] E[Z] − b E[Y] E[Z]
= a{E[XZ] − E[X]E[Z]} + b{E[YZ] − E[Y]E[Z]}
= a Cov(X, Z) + b Cov(Y, Z).

(7.55)

7. By property 6. we have
Var(X + Y) = Cov(X + Y, X + Y) .

(7.56)

By property 5. we can expand this to
Cov(X + Y, X + Y) = Cov(X, X + Y) + Cov(Y, X + Y)
= Cov(X, X) + Cov(X, Y) + Cov(Y, X) + Cov(Y, Y),

(7.57)

where we have also used the symmetry property 4. to expand the second argument of the
covariance. Now, by properties 4. and 6. we can simplify the above sum to Var(X) + Var(Y) +
2Cov(X, Y), which had to be shown.
8. If X and Y are independent, then EXY = EXEY, so that Cov(X, Y) = EXY − EXEY = 0.
□

Positive semidefiniteness. Note that any covariance matrix Σ is a symmetric matrix. In fact, it is

u⊤ Σ u ⩾ 0 .

(7.76)

The Multivariate Normal Distribution

In this section we have a closer look at normally distributed random variables and their properties,
and introduce normally distributed random vectors.
It is helpful to view normally distributed random variables as simple transformations of standard
normal random variables. For example, let X ∼ N(0, 1). Then, X has density fX given by

(7.82)

Indeed, since X1 , . . . , Xn are independent standard normals, EX = 0 and E XX⊤ = I, so by linearity
of expectation EZ = µ + B EX = µ, and by the definition of the covariance matrix,

Cov(Z) = E(Z − µ)(Z − µ)⊤ = E(BX)(BX)⊤ = B E XX⊤ B⊤ = BIB⊤ = BB⊤ = Σ .
(7.83)
Any random vector of the form (7.82) is said to have a jointly normal (or multivariate normal)
distribution. We write Z ∼ N(µ, Σ).
Remark (Standard Definition of the Multivariate Normal). The standard definition in probability theory is more general: a random vector Z has a multivariate normal distribution if and only if t ⊤ Z has
a (univariate) normal distribution for every t ∈ Rn . This definition is equivalent to the constructive
one above when Σ is invertible, but it also covers cases where Σ is singular and no density exists —
for example, the joint distribution of residuals from a normal linear regression. For our purposes
the constructive definition suffices.

The Multinomial Distribution

The multinomial distribution can be viewed as a generalization of the binomial distribution to
experiments with more than two outcomes.

Definition : Multinomial Distribution — We say that (X1 , X2 , . . . , Xk ) has a multinomial distribu-

tion, with parameters n and p1 , p2 , . . . , pk , if

P (X1 = x1 , . . . , Xk = xk ) =

n!

x1 ! x2 ! · · · xk ! 1 2

We independently throw n balls into k urns, such that each ball
is thrown in urn i with probability pi , i = 1, . . . , k; see Figure 7.3.

Figure 7.3: Throwing n balls into k urns.
Let Xi be the total number of balls in urn i, i = 1, . . . , k. We show that (X1 , . . . , Xk ) ∼
Mnom(n, p1 , . . . , pk ). Let x1 , . . . , xk be integers between 0 and n that sum up to n. The probability that the first x1 balls fall in the first urn, the next x2 balls fall in the second urn, etcetera,
is
p1x1 p2x2 · · · pkxk .

(7.96)

To find the probability that there are x1 balls in the first urn, x2 in the second, etcetera, we have
to multiply the probability above with the number of ways in which we can fill the urns with
x1 , x2 , . . . , xk balls, i.e., n!/(x1 ! x2 ! · · · xk !). This gives (7.95).
Remark. Note that for the binomial distribution there are only two possible urns. Also, note that
for each i = 1, . . . , k, Xi ∼ Bin(n, pi ).

The components of a multinomial random vector are negatively correlated: if more balls fall in one
urn, fewer are available for the others. Specifically,

Conditional Expectation and the Law of Total Expectation

Conditional distributions allow us to “update” our beliefs about one random variable given information about another. Conditional expectation extends this idea to expectations: it answers the
question “what is the expected value of Y if we know the value of X?”
Since a conditional pmf (pdf) has all the properties of a probability mass (density) function, it
makes sense to define the corresponding conditional expectation (in the continuous case) as
Z
E[Y | X = x] =
y fY|X (y | x) dy .
(7.100)
For discrete X and Y we have instead:

is that its expectation is simply the expectation of Y. It simplifies the law of total probability calculations for random variables as in (7.29).

The tower property is also called the law of iterated expectations or the law of total expectation. It is an indispensable tool: to compute E[Y], one can first compute E[Y | X = x] as a function
of x, and then average over X.
A fair coin is tossed. If heads, a fair die is rolled and

Random Sums and the Law of Total Variance

A powerful application of conditional expectation is to problems involving a random number of
random variables. In such problems, the tower property and a companion result — the law of total
variance — allow us to compute both the mean and the variance of the resulting sum.

Now let N be a random variable (independent of the Xi ) which takes values in the non-negative

ES N = E(E[S N | N]) = E(Nµ) = µ EN.

This is called Wald’s equation for the mean.
To find the variance of S N , we need a new tool.

The Law of Total Variance
Claim 43: Law of Total Variance — For any pair of random variables X, Y with Var(Y) < ∞,

Var(Y) = E[Var(Y | X)] + Var(E[Y | X]).

(7.110)

Proof: By the computational formula for variance, Var(Y) = E[Y 2 ] − (E[Y])2 . Applying the tower
property to Y 2 and Y:
E[Y 2 ] = E[E[Y 2 | X]],

E[Y] = E[E[Y | X]].

(7.111)

Now, the conditional variance satisfies Var(Y | X) = E[Y 2 | X] − (E[Y | X])2 , so E[Y 2 | X] =
Var(Y | X) + (E[Y | X])2 . Taking expectations:
E[Y 2 ] = E[Var(Y | X)] + E[(E[Y | X])2 ].

(7.112)

Subtracting (E[Y])2 = (E[E[Y | X]])2 :
Var(Y) = E[Var(Y | X)] + E[(E[Y | X])2 ] − (E[E[Y | X]])2 .
|

}

(7.113)

Var(E[Y | X])

□
The two terms have intuitive names. The first, E[Var(Y | X)], is the average unexplained variance — the variability in Y that remains even after knowing X. The second, Var(E[Y | X]), is the
explained variance — how much the conditional mean of Y varies as X changes.

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Variance of a Random Sum
Example (Variance of S N ). Returning to Example 7.9, we apply the law of total variance to find
Var(S N ). Since the Xi are iid with mean µ and variance σ2 , and N is independent of the Xi :

E[S N | N] = Nµ,

Var(S N | N) = Nσ2 .

(7.114)

Applying (7.110):
Var(S N ) = E[Nσ2 ] + Var(Nµ) = σ2 E[N] + µ2 Var(N).

(7.115)

Var(S N ) = σ2 E[N] + µ2 Var(N).

(7.116)

In summary,
This is Wald’s equation for the variance.

Compound Poisson Distribution
An important special case arises when N ∼ Poi(λ).
An insurance company receives claims at a rate of λ per month,
modelled as N ∼ Poi(λ). Each claim amount Xi is iid with mean µ and variance σ2 , independent of
N. The total monthly payout is S N = X1 + · · · + XN .

Example (Compound Poisson).

Since E[N] = Var(N) = λ for a Poisson variable,
E[S N ] = µλ,

Var(S N ) = σ2 λ + µ2 λ = λ(σ2 + µ2 ) = λ E[X12 ].

(7.117)

For instance, with λ = 100 claims per month and claim amounts having mean µ = 500 and standard
deviation σ = 200:
E[S N ] = 500 · 100 = 50,000,

Var(S N ) = 100 · (2002 + 5002 ) = 29,000,000,

giving a standard deviation of approximately 5,385.

CHAPTER

8

M ORE A SPECTS OF J OINT
D ISTRIBUTIONS

In this chapter we study transformations of vector random variables — their densities via the Jacobian, linear maps and their effects on mean and covariance, and the Cholesky decomposition for
generating multivariate normal samples. We then derive distributions of sums, products, ratios, and
order statistics of independent random variables, with convolutions as a central tool.

8.1

Transformations of Vector Random Variables

In Chapter 7 we studied joint distributions and their properties. A natural next question is: if we
know the joint distribution of a random vector X, what can we say about the distribution of a
transformed vector Z = g(X)?
This question arises frequently. For example, given two independent standard normals (X1 , X2 ),
what is the distribution of their polar coordinates (R, Θ)? Or given a random vector with covariance
Σ, what happens to the distribution after a linear transformation Z = AX?
In one dimension, we studied the change-of-variable formula and the CDF method (Section 6.1). In this chapter, we extend these tools to multiple dimensions. The key ingredients are:
• The Jacobian matrix of partial derivatives, which describes how volumes are distorted by
the transformation.
• The transformation rule, which gives the density of Z = g(X) in terms of the density of X
and the Jacobian determinant.
We begin with the general transformation rule, then specialise to the important case of linear transformations.
180

More Aspects of Joint Distributions

8.2

181

The transformation rule has a beautiful application to the bivariate standard normal distribution. By

The Box–Muller Algorithm
Since the inverse probability transform gives V = −2 ln U1 when V ∼ Exp(1/2) and U1 ∼ U(0, 1),
and Θ = 2πU2 when U2 ∼ U(0, 1), the polar decomposition above yields a simple algorithm for
generating standard normal variables.
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

More Aspects of Joint Distributions

183

Algorithm 8.3.1: Generation of a pair of N(0, 1) variables (Box–Muller)
1. Generate two independent random variables, U1 and U2 , from U(0, 1).
2. Return two independent standard normal variables, X and Y, via
X = (−2 ln U1 )1/2 cos(2πU2 ) ,

(8.10)

Y = (−2 ln U1 )1/2 sin(2πU2 ) .

To generate Z ∼ N(µ, σ2 ), simply compute Z = µ + σX, where X is a standard normal obtained from the algorithm above. The Box–Muller method is efficient — it transforms two uniform
random variables into two independent normal random variables with no rejection step.

8.4

Linear Transformations: Effects on Mean and Covariance

Let x = (x1 , . . . , xn ) be a (column) vector in Rn and A an (m × n)-matrix. The mapping x 7→ z, with
z = Ax

(8.11)

is called a linear transformation. Now consider a random vector X = (X1 , . . . , Xn ), and let
Z = AX .

(8.12)

Then Z is a random vector in Rm . Again, in principle, if we know the joint distribution of X then
we can derive the joint distribution of Z. Let us first see how the expectation vector and covariance
matrix are transformed.
Claim 45: Mean and Covariance Under Linear Maps — If X has expectation vector µ X and cov-

ariance matrix Σ X , then the expectation vector and covariance matrix of Z = AX are respectively given
by
µ Z = Aµ X
(8.13)
and
Σ Z = A Σ X A⊤ .

(8.14)

Proof: We have µ Z = EZ = EAX = AEX = Aµ X and
Σ Z = E(Z − µ Z )(Z − µ Z )⊤ = EA(X − µ X )(A(X − µ X ))⊤
= AE(X − µ X )(X − µ X )⊤ A⊤
= A Σ X A⊤
□

which completes the proof.

Density of a Linear Transformation
From now on assume A is an invertible (n × n)-matrix. If X has joint density f X , what is the joint
density f Z of Z?
Consider Figure 8.1. For any fixed x, let z = Ax. Hence, x = A−1 z. Consider the n-dimensional
cube C = [z1 , z1 + h] × · · · × [zn , zn + h]. Let D be the image of C under A−1 , i.e., the parallelepiped
of all points x such that Ax ∈ C. Then,
P (Z ∈ C) ≈ hn f Z (z) .

(8.15)

D

C

A

Figure 8.1: Linear transformation
Now recall from linear algebra that any n-dimensional rectangle with “volume” V is transformed into a n-dimensional parallelepiped with volume V |A|, where |A| := | det(A)|. Thus,
P (Z ∈ C) = P (X ∈ D) ≈ hn |A−1 | f X (x) = hn |A|−1 f X (x)

(8.16)

Letting h go to 0 we conclude that
f Z (z) =

f X (A−1 z)
, z ∈ Rn .
|A|

(8.17)

The determinant |A| is the factor by which
A scales n-dimensional volumes. If A stretches space by |A|, then a small region of volume V in
X-space is mapped to a region of volume |A| V in Z-space. Since the total probability is unchanged,
the same probability mass is now spread over a larger region, so the density must be divided by |A|
to compensate. Conversely, if A compresses space (|A| < 1), the density increases.
Remark (Intuition for the Division by the Determinant).

For example, take
"

#
2 0
A=
,
|A| = 6.
(8.18)
0 3
This stretches the x1 -axis by 2 and the x2 -axis by 3, so a unit square in X-space becomes a 2 × 3
rectangle in Z-space with area 6. The density f Z is therefore f X (A−1 z)/6: the same probability mass
spread over 6 times the area.

Generating Multivariate Normal Distributions

In Chapter 7 we showed that the multivariate normal distribution N(µ, Σ) arises by applying an
affine transformation to independent standard normals. This provides a practical recipe for simulation: given any target mean vector µ and positive definite covariance matrix Σ, we can generate
samples from N(µ, Σ) in two steps.

The Cholesky Decomposition Method
A matrix Σ admits a Cholesky decomposition
Σ = L L⊤ ,

(8.19)

where L is a lower triangular matrix with positive diagonal entries, if and only if Σ is symmetric
positive definite. Moreover, this decomposition is then unique.
A covariance matrix is always symmetric positive semidefinite—that is, a Σ a ⩾ 0 for all a—but need not be positive definite. It is
positive definite exactly when no component of X is an exact affine function of the others. For
symmetric positive semidefinite matrices, positive definiteness is equivalent to invertibility: a zero
eigenvalue makes Σ both singular and merely semidefinite. When Σ is only positive semidefinite
(and hence singular), the Cholesky decomposition does not apply. For example, if X1 ∼ N(0, 1)
and X2 = X1 , then
"
#
1 1
Σ=
,
(8.20)
1 1
which is singular (det Σ = 0) and admits no Cholesky decomposition.
Remark (Positive Semidefiniteness of Covariance Matrices).
⊤

Now let X = (X1 , . . . , Xn ) be a vector of independent standard normal random variables. Define
Z = µ + L X.

(8.21)

By Claim 45, the expectation vector and covariance matrix of Z are
E[Z] = µ + L E[X] = µ,

Σ Z = L I L⊤ = L L⊤ = Σ.

(8.22)

Since Z is an affine transformation of jointly normal random variables, Z ∼ N(µ, Σ).

Distributions of Sums, Products, and Ratios of Independent Random Variables

When X and Y are independent continuous random variables with known densities, we can derive
the density of various combinations — sums, products, and ratios — using the transformation rule
from Section 8.2.

The inverse is X = Z/W, Y = W, and the Jacobian determinant is |∂(x, y)/∂(z, w)| = 1/|w|. By the

8.7

Convolutions

In the previous section we saw formulas for the density of products and ratios. The most important
case — the density of a sum — deserves its own treatment, and leads to the concept of convolution.

finding the pdf of Z = X + Y. By conditioning on X, we have (see (7.29)):

much simpler approach: the MGF of a sum of independent random variables is the product of the
individual MGFs, and the density can then be identified by recognising the resulting MGF.

8.8

Distributions of Maxima, Minima, and Order Statistics

Given a random sample X1 , . . . , Xn from some distribution, we often want to know the distribution
of the largest or smallest observation, or more generally the k-th smallest. These are called order
statistics.

Maximum and Minimum
Suppose X1 , . . . , Xn are independent and have cdf F. Let Y =
min(X1 , . . . , Xn ) and Z = max(X1 , . . . , Xn ). The cdf of Y and Z are easily obtained. First, note that
the maximum of the {Xi } is less than some number z if and only if all Xi are less than z. Thus,
Example (Maximum and Minimum).

P (Z ⩽ z) = P (X1 ⩽ z, X2 ⩽ z, . . . , Xn ⩽ z) = P (X1 ⩽ z)P (X2 ⩽ z) · · · P (Xn ⩽ z),

(8.44)

where the second equation follows from the independence assumption. It follows that
FZ (z) = (F(z))n .

(8.45)

Similarly,
P (Y > y) = P (X1 > y, X2 > y, . . . , Xn > y) = P (X1 > y)P (X2 > y) · · · P (Xn > y),

(8.46)

so that
FY (y) = 1 − (1 − F(y))n .

fY (y) = n (1 − F(y))n−1 f (y).

(8.48)

General Order Statistics
Let X1 , . . . , Xn be iid with cdf F and pdf f . The order statistics are the values arranged in increasing
order:
X(1) ⩽ X(2) ⩽ · · · ⩽ X(n) ,

(8.51)

so that X(1) = min(X1 , . . . , Xn ) and X(n) = max(X1 , . . . , Xn ).
Claim 46: Density of the k-th Order Statistic — The pdf of the k-th order statistic X(k) is

fX(k) (x) =

n!

(k − 1)! (n − k)!

(8.52)

Proof: For X(k) to lie in a small interval [x, x + h), we need exactly k − 1 of the n observations below

F(x), f (x)h, and 1 − F(x) − f (x)h:
P (x ⩽ X(k) < x + h) ≈

n!
(F(x))k−1 f (x) h (1 − F(x))n−k .
(k − 1)! 1! (n − k)!

Dividing by h and letting h → 0 gives the result.

(8.53)

M OMENT G ENERATING F UNCTIONS ,
R ELATED T RANSFORMS , AND E XAMPLES

In this chapter we introduce probability generating functions and moment generating functions —
transforms that encode a distribution and turn convolutions into simple algebra. We develop their
properties, show how to extract probabilities and moments, and apply them to sums of independent
random variables and a branching process model.

9.1

Probability and Moment Generating Functions

Many calculations and manipulations involving probability distributions are facilitated by the use
of transforms. A transform encodes the entire distribution of a random variable into a single function, in such a way that many operations — computing moments, summing independent random
variables, identifying distributions — become algebraically simple. In this section we define the
two most important transforms: the probability generating function and the moment generating
function.

The Probability Generating Function
Definition : Probability Generating Function — Let X be a non-negative and integer-valued random variable. The probability generating function (PGF) of X is the function G : [−1, 1] → R defined
by

in Chapter 3. At z = 1, derivatives are interpreted as left-hand limits (see [7]). Note that G(1) = 1

Uniqueness
The most important theoretical property of both transforms is that they uniquely determine the
distribution.
Claim 47: Uniqueness of the PGF — Two non-negative integer-valued random variables have the

same distribution if and only if they have the same PGF.
Claim 48: Uniqueness of the MGF — If the MGF M(s) exists (i.e., is finite) for all s in some open

interval containing 0, then M uniquely determines the distribution of X. That is, two random variables
with the same MGF have the same distribution.

These uniqueness results are powerful: to show that a random variable has a particular distribution, it suffices to compute its transform and recognise it as the transform of that distribution. We
will use this strategy repeatedly.

9.2

Other Transforms: Laplace-Stieltjes and Characteristic
Functions

The PGF and MGF are the most commonly used transforms in this course, but there are several
related transforms that appear in more advanced work. We briefly survey them here.

The Laplace-Stieltjes Transform
For a non-negative random variable X (not necessarily integer-valued), the Laplace-Stieltjes transform is defined as
b := E[e−sX ], s ⩾ 0.
F(s)
(9.14)
b = M(−s). The Laplace-Stieltjes transform
Note that this is simply the MGF evaluated at −s: F(s)
is preferred in some areas (e.g., queueing theory and reliability) because it is always well-defined
for s ⩾ 0 when X ⩾ 0, whereas the MGF M(s) = E[e sX ] may be infinite for s > 0 if X has a heavy
right tail.

The Characteristic Function
For a random variable X that can take any real value (positive or negative), the MGF may not exist
at all (e.g., for the Cauchy distribution). The characteristic function avoids this problem by using

Basic Properties of Generating Functions

The PGF encodes the entire pmf of a non-negative integer-valued random variable. In this section
we show how to extract probabilities and factorial moments from it.

Retrieving Probabilities from the PGF
Knowing only the PGF of X, we can recover the pmf:
P (X = x) =

Factorial Moments from the PGF
The PGF also provides a convenient route to the moments of X, via the so-called factorial moments. Differentiating G(z) = E[zX ] with respect to z:
G′ (z) = E[XzX−1 ] ,
G′′ (z) = E[X(X − 1)zX−2 ] ,
G′′′ (z) = E[X(X − 1)(X − 2)zX−3 ] ,
et cetera. Evaluating at z = 1 gives the factorial moments:
G(k) (1) = E[X(X − 1)(X − 2) · · · (X − k + 1)] ,

k = 1, 2, 3, . . .

(9.23)

In particular, the first two factorial moments yield the mean and variance:
EX = G′ (1),

(9.24)

and, since E[X(X − 1)] = E[X 2 ] − E[X], we have E[X 2 ] = G′′ (1) + G′ (1), so
Var(X) = G′′ (1) + G′ (1) − (G′ (1))2 .

(9.26)

E[X] = λ,

9.5

Transforms and Convolution Simplification

In Chapter 8 we saw that the distribution of a sum X + Y of independent random variables can be
found by convolving their distributions — an operation that involves summing (or integrating) over
all ways the individual values can add up to a given total. Convolutions are conceptually simple but
computationally tedious: they require nested sums or integrals that quickly become unwieldy.
Transforms turn convolutions into multiplications. This is their single most powerful application.

The Product Rule for PGFs
Claim 49: PGF of a sum of independent random variables — Let X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn be independent
non-negative integer-valued random variables with PGFs G X1 , G X2 , . . . , G Xn . Then the PGF of S n =
X1 + X2 + · · · + Xn is
(9.40)
GS n (z) = G X1 (z) G X2 (z) · · · G Xn (z).

Proof: By definition, GS n (z) = E[zS n ] = E[zX1 +X2 +···+Xn ] = E[zX1 zX2 · · · zXn ]. Since X1 , . . . , Xn are
independent, the expectation of a product equals the product of expectations (Claim 37):
GS n (z) = E[zX1 ] E[zX2 ] · · · E[zXn ] = G X1 (z) G X2 (z) · · · G Xn (z) .

(9.41)
□

The Product Rule for MGFs
Claim 50: MGF of a sum of independent random variables — Let X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn be independent

random variables (not necessarily integer-valued) with MGFs MX1 , MX2 , . . . , MXn . Then the MGF of
S n = X1 + X2 + · · · + Xn is
MS n (s) = MX1 (s) MX2 (s) · · · MXn (s).
(9.42)

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Moment Generating Functions, Related Transforms, and Examples

201

Proof: By definition, MS n (s) = E[e sS n ] = E[e s(X1 +···+Xn ) ] = E[e sX1 e sX2 · · · e sXn ]. Since X1 , . . . , Xn are
independent:
MS n (s) = E[e sX1 ] E[e sX2 ] · · · E[e sXn ] = MX1 (s) MX2 (s) · · · MXn (s) .

(9.43)
□

Remark. For iid random variables X1 , . . . , Xn (all with the same PGF G or MGF M), the results
simplify to GS n (z) = [G(z)]n and MS n (s) = [M(s)]n .
The strategy for finding the distribution of S n is now:
1. Compute the transform of S n using the product rule.
2. Recognise the result as the transform of a known distribution.
3. Conclude (by the uniqueness of the transform) that S n has that distribution.

Continuous Examples
Let X1 , . . . , Xn be iid Exp(λ) random variables. From
Example 9.1, each has MGF M(s) = λ/(λ − s). By the product rule,

From Example 9.1, this is exactly the MGF of the Gamma(n, λ) distribution. By uniqueness,
Example (Sum of iid exponentials is Gamma).

X1 + · · · + Xn ∼ Gamma(n, λ).

(9.45)

This recovers, in one line, the result that the sum of n independent exponential waiting times has a
Gamma distribution (recall Section 5.5) — a fact that required considerably more effort to establish via convolution. More generally, the same argument shows that if X1 ∼ Gamma(α1 , λ) and
X2 ∼ Gamma(α2 , λ) are independent (same rate), then X1 + X2 ∼ Gamma(α1 + α2 , λ): the shape
parameters add.

Discrete Examples
Example (Sum of independent Poisson random variables).

Let X ∼ Poi(λ) and Y ∼ Poi(µ) be

independent. Their PGFs are e−λ(1−z) and e−µ(1−z) , so
G X+Y (z) = e−λ(1−z) · e−µ(1−z) = e−(λ+µ)(1−z) ,

(9.48)

which is the PGF of Poi(λ + µ). Hence
X + Y ∼ Poi(λ + µ).

(9.49)

The Poisson family is closed under addition: the rates simply add.
Example (Sum of iid Bernoullis is Binomial). Let X1 , . . . , Xn be iid Ber(p) random variables. Each
has PGF G(z) = 1 − p + pz. By the product rule,

GS n (z) = (1 − p + pz)n ,

(9.50)

which is the PGF of Bin(n, p). Hence S n = X1 + · · · + Xn ∼ Bin(n, p). This gives a clean proof
that the binomial distribution counts the number of successes in n independent Bernoulli trials.
Similarly, if X ∼ Bin(n, p) and Y ∼ Bin(m, p) are independent, then X + Y ∼ Bin(n + m, p): the
number-of-trials parameters add.
Let X ∼ NegBin(r1 , p) and Y ∼ NegBin(r2 , p)
be independent, where NegBin(r, p) counts the number of failures before the r-th success. The PGF
of NegBin(r, p) is

the total number of failures has a NegBin(r1 + r2 , p) distribution.

9.6

Branching Process Example Using Probability Generating Functions

The Galton–Watson branching process is a simple model of population growth that provides a
beautiful application of probability generating functions and conditional expectation.

The Model
A population evolves in discrete generations. Generation 0 consists of a single individual (the
“ancestor”). Each individual in generation n independently produces a random number of offspring
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Moment Generating Functions, Related Transforms, and Examples

203

according to a common distribution with pmf
pk = P (an individual has k offspring),

k = 0, 1, 2, . . .

(9.53)

These offspring form generation n + 1. Let Zn denote the population size in generation n, so Z0 = 1.
The offspring distribution has PGF
G(z) =

∞
X

pk zk = E[zX ] ,

(9.54)

k=0

where X is the number of offspring of a single individual. We write µ = E[X] = G′ (1) for the mean
number of offspring per individual.

Mean Population Size via Conditional Expectation
We can find E[Zn+1 ] using the tower property (Claim 42). Given Zn = m, generation n + 1 consists
of the offspring of m independent individuals, each producing a random number of offspring with
mean µ. Therefore,
E[Zn+1 | Zn = m] = mµ ,

(9.55)

so E[Zn+1 | Zn ] = µ Zn . Applying the tower property:
E[Zn+1 ] = E[E[Zn+1 | Zn ]] = E[µ Zn ] = µ E[Zn ] .

(9.56)

Since Z0 = 1, iterating gives
E[Zn ] = µn .

(9.57)

The mean population grows geometrically if µ > 1, stays constant if µ = 1, and decays to 0 if
µ < 1.
Figure 9.1 illustrates the difference between the supercritical (µ > 1) and subcritical (µ < 1)
regimes via simulation of a binary splitting process (where each individual either dies or produces
two offspring). In the supercritical case some realisations explode while others go extinct; in the
subcritical case all realisations quickly die out.

The PGF of Zn
Let Gn (z) = E[zZn ] be the PGF of Zn . We claim that Gn is obtained by iterating G:
Gn (z) = G(G(· · · G(z) · · · )) .
|

(9.58)

n times

We write Gn = G ◦ G ◦ · · · ◦ G (n-fold composition).
Proof: We proceed by induction. The base case n = 1 is clear: Z1 = X (the offspring of the single
ancestor), so G1 (z) = G(z).
For the inductive step, suppose Gn (z) is the n-fold composition. In generation n + 1, each of the
Zn individuals independently produces offspring. If Zn = m, then Zn+1 = X1 + X2 + · · · + Xm , where
X1 , . . . , Xm are iid with PGF G. By the product rule (Claim 49),
E[zZn+1 | Zn = m] = [G(z)]m .

(9.59)

(9.60)

where the last step uses the definition of Gn . By the inductive hypothesis, Gn (G(z)) is the (n+1)-fold
composition of G.
□

Extinction Probability
A key question is: what is the probability that the population eventually dies out? Define the
extinction probability
q = P (Zn = 0 for some n ⩾ 1) .

(9.61)

Since {Zn = 0} implies {Zn+1 = 0} (an extinct population stays extinct), the events are nested and
P (Zn = 0) ↑ q as n → ∞.

Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Moment Generating Functions, Related Transforms, and Examples

205

Now, P (Zn = 0) = Gn (0). Taking the limit:
q = lim Gn (0) .

(9.62)

n→∞

One can show that q is the smallest non-negative solution of the fixed-point equation
G(z) = z.

(9.63)

The key result is:
• If µ ⩽ 1: extinction is certain (q = 1), unless p1 = 1 (every individual has exactly one
offspring).
• If µ > 1: there is a positive probability of survival (q < 1). The extinction probability q is the
unique solution of G(z) = z in [0, 1).
The graphical interpretation is simple: plot G(z) and the diagonal y = z on [0, 1]. The extinction
probability is the leftmost intersection point. When µ ⩽ 1, the curve G(z) lies above the diagonal
for all z < 1 and meets it only at z = 1. When µ > 1, the curve crosses the diagonal at some q < 1.
Figure 9.2 illustrates this for the binary splitting and Poisson offspring distributions.

CHAPTER

10

R ANDOM S AMPLES AND L IMIT T HEOREMS

In this chapter we study statistics computed from random samples — the sample mean and sample
variance, their distributions, and their unbiasedness. We then establish the laws of large numbers
and the central limit theorem, which together describe the behaviour of averages and sums for large
samples.

10.1

Random Samples and Statistics

In follow-up courses on statistics, the focus shifts to statistical inference: given observed data,
what can we learn about the underlying process that generated it? In the most basic setup one
has an iid sample X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn from some density f (or probability mass function in the discrete
case). If f depends on unknown parameters θ, the goal of inference is to estimate or test hypotheses
about θ. That is not our concern here. Instead, we study the distributional properties of summary
functions of the sample — these properties form the probabilistic foundation on which all inference
methods rest.
Definition : Random Sample — We call X1 , X2 , . . . , Xn a random sample of size n from a distri-

bution with density (or pmf) f if the Xi are independent and identically distributed (iid), each with
density f .

A statistic is any function T : Rn → R that maps the sample (X1 , . . . , Xn ) to a real number.
Since a statistic is a function of random variables, it is itself a random variable whose distribution
is determined by the joint distribution of the sample. The two most important statistics are the

In mathematical statistics, an estimator whose expectation equals the parameter it targets is
called unbiased. The results above show that X̄n is unbiased for µ and S 2 is unbiased for σ2 . The
divisor n − 1 (rather than n) in S 2 is precisely what achieves this.

10.2

Properties of Normal Samples

When the underlying distribution is normal, we can determine the exact distributions of the sample
mean and sample variance. These results form the backbone of classical methods such as t-tests
and confidence intervals.

Distribution of the Sample Mean
Claim 53: Distribution of the Sample Mean for Normal Populations — If X1 , . . . , Xn are iid

N(µ, σ2 ), then

!
σ2

Proof: Write X̄n = 1n (X1 + · · · + Xn ). Since the Xi are independent normals, their sum S n =
X1 + · · · + Xn is normal by Claim 41. A scalar multiple of a normal random variable is again normal,
so X̄n = S n /n is normal. Its mean and variance are E[X̄n ] = µ and Var(X̄n ) = σ2 /n by Claim 51. □

The Chi-Squared Distribution
To describe the distribution of the sample variance from a normal sample, we first introduce the
chi-squared distribution.
Definition : Chi-Squared Distribution — If Z1 , . . . , Zk are iid N(0, 1), then

Q = Z12 + Z22 + · · · + Zk2

(10.10)

density is

The chi-squared distribution has E[Q] = k and Var(Q) = 2k.

The t-distribution is symmetric about 0 and bell-shaped, but with heavier tails than the normal.
The special case k = 1 gives the Cauchy(0, 1) distribution (see Section 6.4), which has such heavy
tails that its mean does not even exist. As k increases, the tails become lighter and the t(k) distribution converges to N(0, 1). In this sense, the t-distribution interpolates between two extremes: the
very heavy-tailed Cauchy at k = 1 and the light-tailed normal at k = ∞.
Figure 10.2 illustrates this by plotting the 0.975 quantile of t(k) as a function of k. At k = 1
(Cauchy) the quantile is approximately 12.7; it drops rapidly and approaches the standard normal
value of 1.96 from above.

10.3

The Laws of Large Numbers

Let X1 , X2 , . . . be an iid sequence with mean µ and let S n = X1 + · · · + Xn . The law of large numbers
roughly states that X̄n = S n /n is close to µ for large n. There are two versions: the weak law and
the strong law. Both guarantee convergence of the sample mean to the population mean, but they
differ in the mode of convergence — a distinction explored in more advanced probability courses.
An important special case arises when the Xi are indicator random variables of an event A, so
that Xi ∼ Ber(p) with p = P (A). Then S n counts the number of times A occurs in n independent
trials, and the sample mean
Sn
X̄n =
(10.20)
n
is the sample proportion — the fraction of trials in which A occurred. Since µ = E[Xi ] = p, the
law of large numbers tells us that this proportion converges to the true probability p as n → ∞. This
is precisely the frequentist justification for interpreting probability as a long-run relative frequency.

The Weak Law
Claim 55: Weak Law of Large Numbers — If X1 , . . . , Xn are independent and identically distributed

with expectation µ and finite variance σ2 , then for all ε > 0

n
lim P

(10.21)

216

Proof: Apply the Markov inequality (Claim 31) to the nonnegative random variable Z = (S n /n−µ)2
with a = ε2 :
E(S n /n − µ)2
P ((S n /n − µ)2 ⩾ ε2 ) ⩽
(10.22)
ε2
The left-hand side of the above equation can also be written as P (|S n /n − µ| ⩾ ε), and the right-hand
side is equal to the variance of S n /n, which is σ2 /n. Combining gives,
σ2
(10.23)
P (|S n /n − µ| ⩾ ε) ⩽ 2 ,
nε
which is precisely Chebyshev’s inequality (Claim 32) applied to X̄n , for any ε > 0. As n → ∞ the
σ2
□
quotient nε
2 tends to zero, and therefore P (|S n /n − µ| ⩾ ε) goes to zero as well.
The weak law is a statement about convergence in probability: for any fixed tolerance ε > 0,
the probability that X̄n deviates from µ by more than ε vanishes as n → ∞. However, this does not
by itself guarantee that the entire sequence X̄1 , X̄2 , . . . settles down to µ.

The Strong Law
The strong law of large numbers makes a stronger assertion.
Claim 56: Strong Law of Large Numbers — If X1 , X2 , . . . are iid with E[Xi ] = µ, then



Sn
P lim
=µ =1.
n→∞ n

(10.24)

This means that the set of outcomes ω for which S nn(ω) → µ has probability one. Intuitively, if
we were to run a computer simulation, then every simulated path of X̄1 , X̄2 , . . . would converge to
µ. The proof is considerably harder than that of the weak law and is omitted.

Convergence in Probability vs. Almost Sure Convergence
The two laws illustrate two fundamental modes of convergence in probability theory:
• The weak law says X̄n → µ in probability: for every ε > 0, P (|X̄n − µ| ⩾ ε) → 0.
• The strong law says X̄n → µ almost surely (a.s.): with probability 1, the entire sequence
converges.
Almost sure convergence implies convergence in probability, but not conversely. More advanced
probability courses study these and other modes of convergence (such as convergence in distribution and convergence in L p ) systematically.

10.4

The Central Limit Theorem and Its Applications

The Central Limit Theorem says something about the approximate distribution of S n (or X̄n ).
Roughly it says this:
Copyright © 2026

School of Mathematics and Physics, The University of Queensland

Random Samples and Limit Theorems

217

The sum of a large number of iid random variables has approximately a normal distribution.
Here is a more precise statement.
Claim 57: Central Limit Theorem — If X1 , . . . , Xn are independent and identically distributed with

expectation µ and variance σ2 < ∞, then for all x ∈ R,

!
S n − nµ
lim P
√ ⩽ x = Φ(x),
n→∞
σ n

(10.25)

where Φ is the cdf of the standard normal distribution.

The CLT from Two Perspectives
The CLT can be stated equivalently in terms of the sum S n or the sample mean X̄n .
√
Sum perspective. The standardised sum (S n −nµ)/(σ n) (recall the idea of standardisation from
Claim 25) converges in distribution to N(0, 1). This means that for large n,
S n ≈ N(nµ, nσ2 ).

(10.26)

This form is natural when we are interested in the total of n iid contributions.
√
Sample mean perspective. Dividing by n, the standardised sample mean (X̄n − µ)/(σ/ n) converges in distribution to N(0, 1). Equivalently, for large n,
!
σ2
X̄n ≈ N µ,
(10.27)
.
n
This form is natural when we are interested in the average of n iid observations.
Note that the distribution of Xi does not have to be normal, or even continuous — the CLT
applies to any iid sequence with finite variance. The requirement σ2 < ∞ is essential: for the
Cauchy distribution (Section 6.4), which has no finite variance, the sample mean does not converge
to a normal distribution no matter how large n is.

distribution. We clearly see convergence to a bell shaped curve.

Normal Approximation to the Binomial
An important application of the CLT is to the binomial distribution. If X ∼ Bin(n, p), then X =
X1 + · · · + Xn where Xi ∼ Ber(p) are iid. Applying the CLT to this sum:
Claim 58: Normal Approximation to the Binomial — Let X ∼ Bin(n, p). For large n,




 ,
P (X ⩽ k) ≈ Φ p
np(1 − p)

(10.28)

or equivalently, X ≈ N(np, np(1 − p)). As a rule of thumb, the approximation is accurate if both np and
n(1 − p) are larger than 5.

A factory produces light bulbs, each of
which is defective independently with probability p = 0.05. In a batch of n = 200 bulbs, what is
the probability that more than 15 are defective?
Example (Normal approximation to a binomial probability).

Let X ∼ Bin(200, 0.05), so µ = np = 10 and σ2 = np(1 − p) = 9.5. We check: np = 10 > 5 and
n(1 − p) = 190 > 5, so the normal approximation applies.

So there is roughly a 5.2% chance that more than 15 bulbs are defective.

Using the CLT for Sums: An Engineering Example
The CLT is especially useful when we need to find the distribution of a total formed from many
independent contributions, none of which need be normally distributed.
A bridge is designed to carry a maximum load of W = 200 tonnes.
Vehicles cross the bridge one at a time, and the weight of each vehicle is a random variable with
mean µ = 1.8 tonnes and standard deviation σ = 0.6 tonnes. Suppose n = 100 vehicles are on the
bridge simultaneously.
Example (Load on a bridge).

Let S 100 = X1 + · · · + X100 be the total weight. By the CLT,
S 100 ≈ N(nµ, nσ2 ) = N(180, 36) ,

(10.30)
√
so S 100 is approximately normal with mean 180 tonnes and standard deviation 36 = 6 tonnes.
The probability that the load exceeds the design limit is
!
200 − 180
= 1 − Φ(3.33) ≈ 0.0004 .
(10.31)
P (S 100 > 200) ≈ 1 − Φ
6
Although we have no idea what the distribution of individual vehicle weights looks like, the CLT
tells us the total is approximately normal, and the probability of exceeding the limit is very small.

CLT Proof Sketch
We outline the main ideas behind the proof of the CLT using moment generating functions.
Proof: (Sketch) Without loss of generality, assume µ = 0 and σ = 1 (this amounts to replacing
each Xi by (X√i − µ)/σ, which does not change the standardised sum). The goal is to show that the
MGF of S n / n converges to the MGF of the standard normal.

Limitations of the MGF approach. The proof above assumes that the MGF M(s) = E[e sX ]
exists in a neighbourhood of s = 0. This fails for some distributions that have finite variance but
extremely heavy tails. For example, the lognormal distribution has finite moments of all orders, yet
its MGF is infinite for every s > 0. Since the CLT applies to any iid sequence with finite variance,
the MGF-based argument does not cover all cases.
A fully rigorous proof replaces the MGF with the characteristic function φ(t) = E[eitX ], t ∈ R,
which exists for every random variable (not just those with light tails). The Taylor expansion and
limiting argument proceed in essentially the same way, but now the continuity theorem (Lévy’s
continuity theorem) guarantees that pointwise convergence of characteristic functions implies convergence in distribution. This approach is covered in more advanced probability courses.
